Why Does the `test` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `test` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `test`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: when a testing framework demands compiler-magic macros, runtime reflection, or a bloated external runner, writing tests stops feeling immediate.\n\nThe **`test`** lib (`RG-011`) provides polymorphic and type-specific assertions that emit clear `[FAIL]` diagnostics showing expected vs. actual values and exit with code `1` the moment an invariant fails.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`TST`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`test`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/test.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `test` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `test` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `test` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 21.2. Why Does the `posix` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `posix` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `posix`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: flags like `O_CREAT` and `O_NONBLOCK` or syscall numbers differ across Linux, macOS, and CPU architectures (`X86_64` vs `Arm64`), and C codebases solve it with an unreadable `#ifdef` jungle.\n\nThe **`posix`** lib implements Ridgun's 2-layer **Platform Abstraction Layer (PAL)** via tabular pattern matching on `sys os` (`Linux | MacOS | Windows`) and `sys arch` (`X86_64 | Arm64 | RiscV64`), plus descriptor polling (`wait fd` / `PollStatus`), monotonic/wall clocks (`RGF-017`), signal watchers (`RGF-018`), and kernel CSPRNG (`sys random bytes`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`POSIX`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`posix`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/posix.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `posix` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Internal Implementation: Poll & Process Status ADTs\n\nObserve how `posix.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\ntype PollStatus = PollReady Int | PollTimeout | PollError Int\n\ntype ProcStatus = ProcExited Int | ProcTimedOut Int | ProcSignal Int\n```\n\n### 22.4.2. Internal Implementation: Path Safety Validation (VUL-INJ-002 / VUL-INPUT-001)\n\nObserve how `posix.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: is bad posix code Int => Bool\n      is bad posix code c   =\n        c == 0 or c == 10 or c == 13 or c == 59 or c == 96 or c == 124 or c == 38 or c == 36 or c == 39 or c == 34\n\nfn :: has unsafe posix char String Int Int => Bool\n      has unsafe posix char s idx slen =\n        | idx >= slen                           = False\n        | is bad posix code (str char at s idx) = True\n        | otherwise                             = has unsafe posix char s (idx + 1) slen\n\nfn :: is safe posix path len String Int => Bool\n      is safe posix path len path plen =\n        | plen == 0 or plen > 4096 = False\n        | otherwise                = not (has unsafe posix char path 0 plen)\n\nfn :: is safe posix path String => Bool\n      is safe posix path path   =\n        is safe posix path len path (str len path)\n```\n\n### 22.4.3. Internal Implementation: RG-010 Filesystem & Process Operations\n\nObserve how `posix.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: posix mkdir String => Bool\n      posix mkdir path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = False\n        | otherwise                     = (sys mkdir path 493) == 0\n\nfn :: posix chmod String Int  => Bool\n      posix chmod path   mode =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = False\n        | otherwise                     = (sys chmod path mode) == 0\n\nfn :: posix chdir String => Bool\n      posix chdir path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = False\n        | otherwise                     = (sys chdir path) == 0\n\nfn :: px syscall1 path Int String => Int\n      px syscall1 path nr  path   =\n        ptr = px alloc page 4096\n        mfd = px alloc mfd\n        b0  = px raw byte 0\n        _   = px poke mfd ptr (path + b0)\n        rc  = sys call nr ptr 0 0 0 0 0\n        _   = px free page mfd ptr 4096\n        rc\n\nfn :: px syscall2 path Int String String => Int\n      px syscall2 path nr p1 p2 =\n        ptr   = px alloc page 4096\n        mfd   = px alloc mfd\n        b0    = px raw byte 0\n        p2048 = ptr + 2048\n        _     = px poke mfd ptr (p1 + b0)\n        _     = px poke mfd p2048 (p2 + b0)\n        rc    = sys call nr ptr p2048 0 0 0 0\n        _     = px free page mfd ptr 4096\n        rc\n\nfn :: sys rmdir String => Bool\n      sys rmdir path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = False\n        | otherwise                     = (px syscall1 path 84 path) == 0\n\nfn :: sys unlink String => Bool\n      sys unlink path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = False\n        | otherwise                     = (px syscall1 path 87 path) == 0\n```\n\n### 22.4.4. Internal Implementation: Ridgun v0.9.5 Target OS / Arch & Process Probe Helpers\n\nObserve how `posix.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: path separator SysOs   => String\n      path separator Windows =  \"\\\\\"\n      path separator _       =  \"/\"\n\nfn :: null device for SysOs   => String\n      null device for Windows =  \"NUL\"\n      null device for _       =  \"/dev/null\"\n\nfn :: null device => String\n      null device =  null device for (sys os)\n\nfn :: process exists Int => Bool\n      process exists pid =\n        | pid <= 0  = False\n        | otherwise = (sys kill pid 0) == 0\n```\n\n---\n\n## 22.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `posix`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Non-matching `sys os` and `sys arch` branches are statically pruned at compile time (Dead-Code Elimination) by `ridgunc`.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/posix.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test posix.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `posix`\n\n1. **Declare `import posix` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `posix` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `posix`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `posix` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 23**, we will explore the **`io`** lib in full depth (The `io` Lib — Typed `IoResult` Errors, `with timeout` Descriptor I/O, and Processes).\n"}, {"num": "22", "slug": "23-lib-io-timeout-bounded-io-and-processes", "file": "23-lib-io-timeout-bounded-io-and-processes.md", "title": "Chapter 23: The `io` Lib — Typed `IoResult` Errors, `with timeout` Descriptor I/O, and Processes", "short_title": "The `io` Lib — Typed `IoResult` Errors, `with timeout` Descriptor I/O, and Processes", "markdown": "# Chapter 23: The `io` Lib — Typed `IoResult` Errors, `with timeout` Descriptor I/O, and Processes\n\n## 23.1. Why Does the `io` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `io` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `io`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: an unbounded `read()` call or child process execution with no deadline is an open invitation for one stalled peer to exhaust every worker in your server.\n\nThe **`io`** lib models every system operation through explicit ADTs (`IoError`, `IoResult[A]`), pairs non-blocking `ppoll` with Ridgun's lexical `with timeout wait_ms do` quotas, and exports the `read fd ... timeout` and `write fd ... timeout` mixfix operators.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`IO`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`io`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/io.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `io` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `io` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `io` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 23.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `io` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`io`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/io.rg` (domain `IO`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/io.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import io` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/io.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libio.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import io` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 23.2.1. Empty pipe read with 50 ms timeout must time out within [40, 300) ms (TS-PERF-001 AC2)\n        t3     = sys monotonic ms\n        r_to   = read fd rfd max 32 timeout 50\n        t4     = sys monotonic ms\n        dt     = t4 - t3\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result r_to) \"err:timeout after 50 ms\" \"empty pipe read Timeout 50\"\n        _      = assert true (dt >= 40 and dt < 300) \"dt in [40, 300): {dt}\"\n\n        -- 2. Close write end and verify subsequent read returns Eof\n        _      = posix close wfd\n        r_eof  = read fd rfd max 64 timeout 100\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result r_eof) \"err:unexpected end of file\" \"closed pipe read returns Eof\"\n        _      = posix close rfd\n\n        -- 5. Bad fd / invalid max_bytes\n        r_bad  = read fd (0 - 1) max 64 timeout 50\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result r_bad) \"err:syscall error 9 in read\" \"negative fd read SysErr 9\"\n        r_zero = read fd rfd max 0 timeout 50\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result r_zero) \"err:syscall error 22 in read\" \"zero max_bytes read SysErr 22\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 23.3.5. Example 5: Safe Subprocess Execution with Strict Deadlines (`io exec timeout`) and Automatic Child Cleanup\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `io` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test io exec timeout => ()\n      test io exec timeout =\n        ok_res = io exec timeout \"true\" 500\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result ok_res) \"ok:int:0\" \"io exec timeout true succeeds\"\n        to_res = io exec timeout \"sleep 2\" 40\n        _      = assert str eq (format io result to_res) \"err:timeout after 40 ms\" \"io exec timeout sleep 2 times out\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 23.3.6. Under the Hood: How `io` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/io.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/io.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 23.4.1. Internal Implementation: Extensible I/O Error & Result Sum Types (§5.1)\n\nObserve how `io.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\ntype IoError = Timeout Int | Eof | SysErr Int String | NotFound String\n\ntype IoResult = String | Int | Timeout Int | Eof | SysErr Int String | NotFound String | IoErr IoError | IoOk String | IoTimeout | IoEof | IoError Int\n\nfn :: format io error IoError            => String\n      format io error (Timeout ms)       =  \"timeout after {ms} ms\"\n      format io error Eof                =  \"unexpected end of file\"\n      format io error (SysErr c op_name) =  \"syscall error {c} in {op_name}\"\n      format io error (NotFound path)    =  \"file not found: {path}\"\n      format io error _                  =  \"unknown io error\"\n\nfn :: format io result IoResult           => String\n      format io result (String s)         =  \"ok:str:{s}\"\n      format io result (IoOk s)           =  \"ok:str:{s}\"\n      format io result (Int n)            =  \"ok:int:{n}\"\n      format io result (Timeout ms)       =  \"err:timeout after {ms} ms\"\n      format io result IoTimeout          =  \"err:timeout\"\n      format io result Eof                =  \"err:unexpected end of file\"\n      format io result IoEof              =  \"err:unexpected end of file\"\n      format io result (IoError c)        =  \"err:io error {c}\"\n      format io result (SysErr c op_name) =  \"err:syscall error {c} in {op_name}\"\n      format io result (NotFound path)    =  \"err:file not found: {path}\"\n      format io result (IoErr err)        =  \"err:\" + (format io error err)\n      format io result _                  =  \"err:unknown io error\"\n```\n\n### 23.4.2. Internal Implementation: Timeout-Bounded Command Execution (§5.2)\n\nObserve how `io.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: has null char String Int Int => Bool\n      has null char s idx slen =\n        | idx >= slen              = False\n        | (str char at s idx) == 0 = True\n        | otherwise                = has null char s (idx + 1) slen\n\nfn :: pad ms three Int => String\n      pad ms three rem =\n        | rem < 10  = \"00{rem}\"\n        | rem < 100 = \"0{rem}\"\n        | otherwise = \"{rem}\"\n\nfn :: format timeout sec Int => String\n      format timeout sec ms  =\n        secs = ms / 1000\n        rem  = ms % 1000\n        p    = pad ms three rem\n        \"{secs}.{p}s\"\n\nfn :: map exec rc Int Int        => IoResult\n      map exec rc rc  timeout_ms =\n        | rc == 124 or rc == 137 = Timeout timeout_ms\n        | rc < 0                 = SysErr (0 - rc) \"exec\"\n        | otherwise              = Int rc\n\nfn :: io exec timeout String Int        => IoResult\n      io exec timeout cmd    timeout_ms =\n        | (str len cmd) == 0 or (has null char cmd 0 (str len cmd)) = SysErr 22 \"exec\"\n        | timeout_ms <= 0                                           = Timeout timeout_ms\n        | otherwise                                                 =\n          tspec = format timeout sec timeout_ms\n          rc    = shell exec \"timeout -k 0.01s {tspec} sh -c \\\"{cmd}\\\" >/dev/null 2>&1\"\n          map exec rc rc timeout_ms\n\nfn :: exec with timeout String Int        => IoResult\n      exec with timeout cmd    timeout_ms =\n        io exec timeout cmd timeout_ms\n```\n\n### 23.4.3. Internal Implementation: Safe File Read & Line-Stream Helpers\n\nObserve how `io.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: io read file safe String => IoResult\n      io read file safe path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path)        = SysErr 22 \"read_file\"\n        | not (lstat exists (file lstat path)) = NotFound path\n        | otherwise                            = String (read file path)\n\nfn :: split lines loop String Int Int Int => [String]\n      split lines loop s idx start slen =\n        | idx >= slen =\n          | start == slen = []\n          | otherwise     = [str slice s start slen] |.\n        | (str char at s idx) == 10 =\n          line = str slice s start idx\n          [line] :: (split lines loop s (idx + 1) (idx + 1) slen)\n        | otherwise =\n          split lines loop s (idx + 1) start slen\n\nfn :: split non empty lines String Int => [String]\n      split non empty lines raw rlen =\n        | rlen == 0 = []\n        | otherwise = split lines loop raw 0 0 rlen\n\nfn :: io read lines String => [String]\n      io read lines path   =\n        | not (is safe posix path path)        = []\n        | not (lstat exists (file lstat path)) = []\n        | otherwise                            =\n          raw = read file path\n          split non empty lines raw (str len raw)\n\nfn :: str list is empty [String] => Bool\n      str list is empty []       =  True\n      str list is empty (_:_)    =  False\n\nfn :: join lines nl [String]    => String\n      join lines nl []          =  \"\"\n      join lines nl (line:rest) =\n        | str list is empty rest = line + \"\\n\"\n        | otherwise              = line + \"\\n\" + (join lines nl rest)\n\nfn :: io write lines String [String] => Int\n      io write lines path   lines    =\n        | not (is safe posix path path) = 0 - 22\n        | otherwise                     =\n          _ = write file path (join lines nl lines)\n          0\n```\n\n### 23.4.4. Internal Implementation: Ridgun v0.9.5 PAL & Ambient Deadline `io read with timeout` (§3.1)\n\nObserve how `io.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: io wrap chunk String => IoResult\n      io wrap chunk \"\"     =  IoEof\n      io wrap chunk s      =  IoOk s\n\nfn :: io read after poll Int Int => IoResult\n      io read after poll fd ev =\n        | ev == 0 =\n          _ = sys close fd\n          IoTimeout\n        | ev < 0 =\n          _ = sys close fd\n          IoError ev\n        | otherwise =\n          chunk = sys read fd fd 65536\n          _     = sys close fd\n          io wrap chunk chunk\n\nfn :: io read poll step Int => IoResult\n      io read poll step fd  =\n        | fd < 0    = IoError fd\n        | otherwise =\n          ev = sys poll fd 1 (0 - 1)\n          io read after poll fd ev\n\nfn :: io read with timeout String Int => IoResult\n      io read with timeout path wait_ms =\n        with timeout wait_ms do\n          fd = sys open path 2048 0\n          io read poll step fd\n```\n\n---\n\n## 23.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `io`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: No read, write, or child command executed through `io` with a `wait_ms` quota can ever block the calling thread or actor indefinitely.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/io.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test io.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `io`\n\n1. **Declare `import io` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `io` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `io`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `io` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 24**, we will explore the **`crypto`** lib in full depth (The `crypto` Lib — SHA-256, HMAC, PBKDF2, Argon2id, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD).\n"}, {"num": "23", "slug": "24-lib-crypto-sha256-hmac-pbkdf2-and-aead", "file": "24-lib-crypto-sha256-hmac-pbkdf2-and-aead.md", "title": "Chapter 24: The `crypto` Lib — SHA-256, HMAC, PBKDF2, Argon2id, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD", "short_title": "The `crypto` Lib — SHA-256, HMAC, PBKDF2, Argon2id, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD", "markdown": "# Chapter 24: The `crypto` Lib — SHA-256, HMAC, PBKDF2, Argon2id, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD\n\n## 24.1. Why Does the `crypto` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `crypto` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `crypto`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: comparing an HMAC tag with `==` opens the door to timing side-channel attacks, while linking `libssl.so` just to compute `SHA-256` or encrypt with `ChaCha20-Poly1305` ruins static binary portability.\n\nThe **`crypto`** lib implements in 100% pure Ridgun over pre-allocated `Tensor[Int]` state: raw byte/bitwise primitives (`RGF-019`), **FIPS 180-4 SHA-256**, **RFC 2104 HMAC-SHA-256**, constant-time equality (`ct equal`), midstate-accelerated **RFC 8018 PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256**, 5-argument **Argon2id**, and **RFC 8439 ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD** (`aead seal` / `aead open`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`CRYPTO`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`crypto`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/crypto.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `crypto` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `crypto` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `crypto` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 24.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `crypto` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`crypto`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/crypto.rg` (domain `CRYPTO`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/crypto.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import crypto` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/crypto.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libcrypto.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import crypto` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 24.2.1. Summary of `crypto` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `sha256 bytes / sha256 hex` | FIPS 180-4 32-byte binary digest or 64-char lowercase hex digest |\n| `hmac sha256 / hmac sha256 hex / ct equal` | RFC 2104 keyed MAC and constant-time accumulator comparison |\n| `pbkdf2 hmac sha256 : String -> String -> Int -> Int -> String` | RFC 8018 key derivation with precomputed inner/outer SHA-256 midstates |\n| `argon2id hash / argon2id verify` | 5-argument RFC 9106 PHC password hashing and constant-time verification |\n| `aead seal / aead open` | RFC 8439 ChaCha20-Poly1305 authenticated encryption and tag-verified decryption |\n\n### 24.2.2. Deep Dive into `crypto`: Step-by-Step Examples for Every Feature\n\nA signature table alone is never enough: let's explore **every capability** of the `crypto` lib through progressive, runnable Ridgun examples. We will start with two everyday tabular recipes and then walk through every functional scenario, transformation, and boundary condition verified in `src/test/ridgun/test crypto.rg`.\n\n### 24.3.1. Example 1: Core Tabular Workflow with `crypto`\n\nNotice how in Ridgun the `=` signs and transformation steps using `crypto` align into a clean, scannable table without unnecessary mutable variables or hidden side effects:\n\n```ridgun\nimport crypto\n\nfn :: cifrar mensaje sesion (secreto : String) (texto : String) => (Int, String)\n      cifrar mensaje sesion secreto texto =\n        key32   = sha256 bytes secreto\n        nonce12 = \"0123456789ab\"\n        aad     = \"canal-autenticado\"\n        sellado = aead seal key32 nonce12 aad texto\n        aead open key32 nonce12 aad sellado\n```\n\n### 24.3.2. Example 2: Safe Composition and Boundary Handling\n\nEvery function in the `crypto` lib is designed to compose cleanly with boolean guards `|` and with the rest of the 32 libs in `ridgun-libs`:\n\n```ridgun\nimport crypto\n\nfn :: verificar firma webhook (clave : String) (cuerpo : String) (firma_hex : String) => Bool\n      verificar firma webhook clave cuerpo firma_hex =\n        esperada = hmac sha256 hex clave cuerpo\n        ct equal esperada firma_hex\n```\n\n### 24.3.3. Example 3: Raw Byte `0..255` Manipulation (`byte str`, `bytes from list`, `bytes to list`) and 32-Bit Unsigned Bitwise Ops (`band`, `bor`, `bxor`, `shl`, `shr`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `crypto` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test rgf019 bytes and bitops => ()\n      test rgf019 bytes and bitops =\n        -- 1. Raw byte_str lengths, wraparound normalization, and full 0..255 round-trip\n        _    = assert int equals (str len (byte str 0)) 1 \"len byte_str 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (str len (byte str 127)) 1 \"len byte_str 127\"\n        _    = assert int equals (str len (byte str 128)) 1 \"len byte_str 128\"\n        _    = assert int equals (str len (byte str 255)) 1 \"len byte_str 255\"\n        _    = assert str eq (byte str (0 - 1)) (byte str 255) \"byte_str -1 wraps to 255\"\n        _    = assert str eq (byte str 256) (byte str 0) \"byte_str 256 wraps to 0\"\n        b5   = bytes from list [0, 1, 127, 128, 255]\n        _    = assert int equals (str len b5) 5 \"len b5 == 5\"\n        rt   = bytes to list b5\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int rt 0) 0 \"rt 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int rt 1) 1 \"rt 1\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int rt 2) 127 \"rt 2\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int rt 3) 128 \"rt 3\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int rt 4) 255 \"rt 4\"\n        b256 = bytes from list (range bytes 0 255)\n        _    = assert int equals (str len b256) 256 \"len b256 == 256\"\n        r256 = bytes to list b256\n        _    = assert int equals (c list len r256) 256 \"r256 list len == 256\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int r256 0) 0 \"r256[0] == 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int r256 128) 128 \"r256[128] == 128\"\n        _    = assert int equals (c nth int r256 255) 255 \"r256[255] == 255\"\n\n        -- 2. RGF-019 32-bit unsigned bitwise operations and boundary shifts\n        _    = assert int equals (band 4294967295 255) 255 \"band\"\n        _    = assert int equals (band 305419896 0) 0 \"band with 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (bor 240 15) 255 \"bor\"\n        _    = assert int equals (bor 305419896 0) 305419896 \"bor with 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (bxor 305419896 4294967295) 3989547399 \"bxor\"\n        _    = assert int equals (bxor 305419896 305419896) 0 \"bxor self == 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shl 1 0) 1 \"shl 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shl 1 (0 - 1)) 0 \"shl negative shift\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shl 1 31) 2147483648 \"shl 31\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shl 1 32) 0 \"shl 32\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shr 2147483648 0) 2147483648 \"shr 0\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shr 2147483648 (0 - 1)) 0 \"shr negative shift\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shr 2147483648 31) 1 \"shr 31\"\n        _    = assert int equals (shr 2147483648 32) 0 \"shr 32\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 24.3.4. Example 4: Official Vectors for SHA-256 (FIPS 180-4), HMAC-SHA-256 (RFC 4231), Constant-Time Equality (`ct equal`), and PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256 (RFC 6070)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `crypto` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test sha256 hmac and pbkdf2 => ()\n      test sha256 hmac and pbkdf2 =\n        -- 1. SHA-256 (empty string \"\" and \"abc\" -> FIPS 180-4 vectors)\n        h_emp = sha256 hex \"\"\n        _     = assert str eq h_emp \"e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855\" \"sha256_hex empty\"\n        h_hex = sha256 hex \"abc\"\n        _     = assert str eq h_hex \"ba7816bf8f01cfea414140de5dae2223b00361a396177a9cb410ff61f20015ad\" \"sha256_hex abc\"\n        h_raw = sha256 bytes \"abc\"\n        _     = assert eq (str len h_raw) 32\n        _     = assert str eq (bytes to hex str h_raw) h_hex \"sha256_bytes matches sha256_hex\"\n\n        -- 2. HMAC-SHA-256 (RFC 4231 Test Case 2 and >64-byte key reduction)\n        mac   = hmac sha256 \"Jefe\" \"what do ya want for nothing?\"\n        _     = assert eq (str len mac) 32\n        _     = assert str eq (bytes to hex str mac) \"5bdcc146bf60754e6a042426089575c75a003f089d2739839dec58b964ec3843\" \"RFC 4231 Test Case 2\"\n        long_k = \"0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef_extra_key_bytes\"\n        mac_lk = hmac sha256 long_k \"message\"\n        _      = assert eq (str len mac_lk) 32\n        _      = assert true (ct equal mac_lk (hmac sha256 (sha256 bytes long_k) \"message\")) \"HMAC >64B key hashed to 32B\"\n\n        -- 3. Constant-time comparison (`ct_equal`)\n        _     = assert true (ct equal \"\" \"\") \"ct_equal empty strings\"\n        _     = assert true (ct equal mac mac) \"ct_equal identical\"\n        _     = assert false (ct equal mac (sha256 bytes \"abc\")) \"ct_equal different 32B\"\n        _     = assert false (ct equal \"short\" \"longer\") \"ct_equal different lengths\"\n\n        -- 4. PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA-256 (RFC 6070 c=1, c=2, multi-block dk_len=64, sub-block dk_len=16, invalid args)\n        dk1   = pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 1 32\n        _     = assert eq (str len dk1) 32\n        _     = assert str eq (bytes to hex str dk1) \"120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837a86548c92ccc35480805987cb70be17b\" \"RFC 6070 PBKDF2 c=1\"\n        dk2   = pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 2 32\n        _     = assert str eq (bytes to hex str dk2) \"ae4d0c95af6b46d32d0adff928f06dd02a303f8ef3c251dfd6e2d85a95474c43\" \"RFC 6070 PBKDF2 c=2\"\n        dk16  = pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 1 16\n        _     = assert eq (str len dk16) 16\n        _     = assert str eq (bytes to hex str dk16) \"120fb6cffcf8b32c43e7225256c4f837\" \"PBKDF2 16-byte prefix\"\n        dk64  = pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 1 64\n        _     = assert eq (str len dk64) 64\n        _     = assert str eq (byte slice dk64 0 32) dk1 \"PBKDF2 64-byte block 1 matches dk1\"\n        _     = assert str eq (pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 0 32) \"\" \"PBKDF2 iterations=0 rejected\"\n        _     = assert str eq (pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"password\" \"salt\" 1 0) \"\" \"PBKDF2 dk_len=0 rejected\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 24.3.5. Example 5: 5-Argument Argon2id Password Hashing (`argon2id hash`, `argon2id verify`) and Memory-Hard Verification (RFC 9106)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `crypto` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test argon2id five arg and perf => ()\n      test argon2id five arg and perf =\n        phc = argon2id hash \"correct horse battery staple\" \"0123456789abcdef\" 64 2 1\n        _   = assert true (str starts with phc \"$argon2id$v=19$m=64,t=2,p=1$\") \"PHC header format\"\n        _   = assert true (argon2id verify \"correct horse battery staple\" phc) \"argon2id_verify matching (pwd, phc)\"\n        _   = assert false (argon2id verify \"wrong password\" phc) \"argon2id_verify wrong password\"\n        _   = assert false (argon2id verify \"correct horse battery staple\" \"$argon2id$v=19$m=64,t=2,p=1$bad$bad\") \"argon2id_verify bad PHC\"\n\n        -- Negative parameter guards for 5-arg argon2id_hash\n        _   = assert str eq (argon2id hash \"password\" \"short\" 64 2 1) \"\" \"salt < 8 bytes rejected\"\n        _   = assert str eq (argon2id hash \"password\" \"0123456789abcdef\" 4 2 1) \"\" \"m_cost < 8*p rejected\"\n        _   = assert str eq (argon2id hash \"password\" \"0123456789abcdef\" 64 0 1) \"\" \"t_cost < 1 rejected\"\n        _   = assert str eq (argon2id hash \"password\" \"0123456789abcdef\" 64 2 0) \"\" \"p_cost < 1 rejected\"\n\n        -- RG-050 / RG-051: Verify 100% in-process pure-Ridgun Argon2id and PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256\n        -- (OpenSSL 3.5 / RFC 9106 vectors + 4096-iteration PBKDF2 vector)\n        phc_s       = argon2id hash \"correct horse\" \"saltsaltsaltsalt\" 64 2 1\n        argon_exact = phc_s == \"$argon2id$v=19$m=64,t=2,p=1$c2FsdHNhbHRzYWx0c2FsdA$5jdMIzWoFbzSOl6k3wai9uU3f8LD+HmGXQgo1uhHQPI\"\n        ok_s        = argon2id verify \"correct horse\" phc_s\n        bad_s       = argon2id verify \"wrong horse\" phc_s\n        bad_empty   = argon2id verify \"correct horse\" \"$argon2id$v=19$m=64,t=2,p=1$c2FsdHNhbHRzYWx0c2FsdA$\"\n        phc_ok      = str starts with phc_s \"$argon2id$v=19$m=64,t=2,p=1$\"\n        pb4k        = bytes to hex str (pbkdf2 hmac sha256 \"correct horse battery staple\" \"0123456789abcdef\" 4096 32)\n        _           = assert true ok_s \"Solstice RGF-015 argon_ok\"\n        _           = assert false bad_s \"Solstice RGF-015 argon_bad\"\n        _           = assert false bad_empty \"RG-051 reject PHC with empty tag\"\n        _           = assert true phc_ok \"Solstice RGF-015 phc prefix\"\n        _           = assert true argon_exact \"Solstice RGF-015 exact OpenSSL 3.5 / libsodium PHC\"\n        _           = assert str eq pb4k \"b87f4b27148d3b05ad7985e3df997f0d9afa6e768e6384555bab71ba10f0c1a5\" \"Solstice RGF-015 PBKDF2 4096\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 24.3.6. Example 6: ChaCha20 Stream Cipher, Poly1305 Authenticator, and RFC 8439 ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD Seal & Open\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `crypto` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test rfc8439 chacha20 poly1305 aead => ()\n      test rfc8439 chacha20 poly1305 aead =\n        key   = bytes from list (range bytes 128 159)\n        nonce = bytes from list [7, 0, 0, 0, 64, 65, 66, 67, 68, 69, 70, 71]\n        aad   = bytes from list [80, 81, 82, 83, 192, 193, 194, 195, 196, 197, 198, 199]\n        pt    = \"Ladies and Gentlemen of the class of '99: If I could offer you only one tip for the future, sunscreen would be it.\"\n\n        sealed = aead seal key nonce aad pt\n        _      = assert eq (str len sealed) ((str len pt) + 16)\n\n        -- Verify first 16 ciphertext bytes and 16-byte Poly1305 tag match RFC 8439 §2.8.2 bit-for-bit\n        ct_prefix_hex = bytes to hex str (byte slice sealed 0 16)\n        tag_hex       = bytes to hex str (byte slice sealed 114 130)\n        _             = assert str eq ct_prefix_hex \"d31a8d34648e60db7b86afbc53ef7ec2\" \"RFC 8439 §2.8.2 ciphertext prefix\"\n        _             = assert str eq tag_hex \"1ae10b594f09e26a7e902ecbd0600691\" \"RFC 8439 §2.8.2 Poly1305 tag\"\n\n        -- Verify aead_open succeeds with (0, pt)\n        res_ok   = aead open key nonce aad sealed\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_ok) 0\n        _        = assert str eq (aead pt res_ok) pt \"aead_open decrypted plaintext\"\n\n        -- Verify empty plaintext and empty AAD round-trip\n        sealed_empty = aead seal key nonce \"\" \"\"\n        _            = assert eq (str len sealed_empty) 16\n        res_empty    = aead open key nonce \"\" sealed_empty\n        _            = assert eq (aead rc res_empty) 0\n        _            = assert str eq (aead pt res_empty) \"\" \"aead_open empty plaintext\"\n\n        -- Verify tamper detection (-12 AUTH_FAILED) on modified AAD, modified nonce, modified ciphertext, or modified tag\n        res_aad  = aead open key nonce \"tampered-aad\" sealed\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_aad) (0 - 12)\n        _        = assert str eq (aead pt res_aad) \"\" \"tampered AAD returns empty plaintext\"\n\n        bad_nonce = bytes from list [8, 0, 0, 0, 64, 65, 66, 67, 68, 69, 70, 71]\n        res_nonce = aead open key bad_nonce aad sealed\n        _         = assert eq (aead rc res_nonce) (0 - 12)\n        _         = assert str eq (aead pt res_nonce) \"\" \"tampered nonce returns empty plaintext\"\n\n        bad_ct   = (byte str 0) + (byte slice sealed 1 130)\n        res_ct   = aead open key nonce aad bad_ct\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_ct) (0 - 12)\n        _        = assert str eq (aead pt res_ct) \"\" \"tampered ciphertext byte returns empty plaintext\"\n\n        bad_seal = (byte slice sealed 0 129) + (byte str 0)\n        res_tag  = aead open key nonce aad bad_seal\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_tag) (0 - 12)\n        _        = assert str eq (aead pt res_tag) \"\" \"tampered tag returns empty plaintext\"\n\n        -- Verify invalid key/nonce/ciphertext length returns (-7, \"\") or \"\"\n        _        = assert str eq (aead seal \"short-key\" nonce aad pt) \"\" \"aead_seal invalid key length\"\n        _        = assert str eq (aead seal key \"short-nonce\" aad pt) \"\" \"aead_seal invalid nonce length\"\n        res_inv  = aead open \"short-key\" nonce aad sealed\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_inv) (0 - 7)\n        res_ninv = aead open key \"short-nonce\" aad sealed\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_ninv) (0 - 7)\n        res_tinv = aead open key nonce aad \"short-tag\"\n        _        = assert eq (aead rc res_tinv) (0 - 7)\n        ()\n```\n\n---\n\n## 24.4. Under the Hood: How `crypto` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/crypto.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/crypto.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 24.4.1. Internal Implementation: RGF-019 Raw Byte Manipulation (`byte_str`, `bytes_from_list`, `bytes_to_list`)\n\nObserve how `crypto.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: norm byte mod Int => Int\n      norm byte mod m   =\n        | m < 0     = m + 256\n        | otherwise = m\n\nfn :: norm byte Int => Int\n      norm byte b   =  norm byte mod (b % 256)\n\nfn :: byte str norm Int => String\n      byte str norm ub  =\n        | ub >= 1 and ub <= 127 = char from code ub\n        | otherwise =\n          efd = sys call 290 (256 + ub) 526336 0 0 0 0\n          s   = sys read fd efd 8\n          _   = sys close efd\n          str slice s 0 1\n\nfn :: byte str Int => String\n      byte str n   =\n        byte str norm (norm byte n)\n\nfn :: bytes from list [Int]    => String\n      bytes from list []       =  \"\"\n      bytes from list (b:rest) =  (byte str b) + (bytes from list rest)\n\nfn :: bytes to list loop String Int Int => [Int]\n      bytes to list loop s idx slen =\n        | idx >= slen = []\n        | otherwise   = [norm byte (str char at s idx)] :: (bytes to list loop s (idx + 1) slen)\n\nfn :: bytes to list String => [Int]\n      bytes to list s      =\n        bytes to list loop s 0 (str len s)\n```\n\n### 24.4.2. Internal Implementation: Hex & List Utilities\n\nObserve how `crypto.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: hextable Int => String\n      hextable _   =  \"0123456789abcdef\"\n\nfn :: crypto byte to hex Int => String\n      crypto byte to hex b   =\n        ub = norm byte b\n        hi = (ub / 16) % 16\n        lo = ub % 16\n        (str slice (hextable 0) hi (hi + 1)) + (str slice (hextable 0) lo (lo + 1))\n\nfn :: bytes to hex str String => String\n      bytes to hex str raw    =\n        bytes list to hex (bytes to list raw)\n\nfn :: bytes list to hex [Int]    => String\n      bytes list to hex []       =  \"\"\n      bytes list to hex (b:rest) =  (crypto byte to hex b) + (bytes list to hex rest)\n\nfn :: crypto hex val Int => Int\n      crypto hex val c   =\n        | c >= 48 and c <= 57  = c - 48\n        | c >= 97 and c <= 102 = (c - 97) + 10\n        | c >= 65 and c <= 70  = (c - 65) + 10\n        | otherwise            = 0\n\nfn :: hex str to bytes loop String Int Int => [Int]\n      hex str to bytes loop h idx hlen =\n        | (idx + 1) >= hlen = []\n        | otherwise         =\n          hi = crypto hex val (str char at h idx)\n          lo = crypto hex val (str char at h (idx + 1))\n          [(hi * 16) + lo] :: (hex str to bytes loop h (idx + 2) hlen)\n\nfn :: hex to byte str String => String\n      hex to byte str h      =\n        bytes from list (hex str to bytes loop h 0 (str len h))\n\nfn :: c list len [Int]    => Int\n      c list len []       =  0\n      c list len (_:rest) =  1 + (c list len rest)\n\nfn :: c nth int [Int]    Int => Int\n      c nth int []       _   =  0\n      c nth int (x:rest) idx =\n        | idx <= 0  = x\n        | otherwise = c nth int rest (idx - 1)\n```\n\n### 24.4.3. Internal Implementation: RGF-015 Pure-Ridgun RFC 9106 Argon2id (`argon2id_hash`, `argon2id_verify`)\n\nObserve how `crypto.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ca2 take ints [Int]    Int => [Int]\n      ca2 take ints []       _   =  []\n      ca2 take ints (x:rest) n   =\n        | n <= 0    = []\n        | otherwise = [x] :: (ca2 take ints rest (n - Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `crypto`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: `aead open` verifies the 16-byte Poly1305 authentication tag in constant time before releasing a single byte of plaintext (returning `(-1, \"\")` on tamper).\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/crypto.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test crypto.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `crypto`\n\n1. **Declare `import crypto` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `crypto` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `crypto`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `crypto` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 25**, we will explore the **`argon2id`** lib in full depth (The `argon2id` Lib — RFC 9106 Argon2id on `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]`, Blake2b, and OWASP Presets).\n"}, {"num": "24", "slug": "25-lib-argon2id-rfc9106-password-hashing", "file": "25-lib-argon2id-rfc9106-password-hashing.md", "title": "Chapter 25: The `argon2id` Lib — RFC 9106 Argon2id on `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]`, Blake2b, and OWASP Presets", "short_title": "The `argon2id` Lib — RFC 9106 Argon2id on `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]`, Blake2b, and OWASP Presets", "markdown": "# Chapter 25: The `argon2id` Lib — RFC 9106 Argon2id on `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]`, Blake2b, and OWASP Presets\n\n## 25.1. Why Does the `argon2id` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `argon2id` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `argon2id`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: hashing passwords with plain SHA-256 lets a modern GPU brute-force billions of guesses per second, yet linking external C libraries for Argon2id often brings FFI and build headaches.\n\nThe **`argon2id`** lib implements **RFC 9106 Argon2id** and **RFC 7693 Blake2b-512** in 100% pure Ridgun directly over native `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]` blocks (1,024 contiguous bytes per block in RMM2 memory), complete with **PHC** string formatting (`$argon2id$v=19$m=...,t=...,p=...$`), **OWASP** presets (`owaspfirst`, `owaspsecond`), secret pepper/associated-data context (`argon2id hash ctx`), raw key derivation (`argon2id derive key`), and parameter upgrade detection (`argon2id needs rehash`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`CRYPTO`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`argon2id`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/argon2id.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `argon2id` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `argon2id` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `argon2id` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 25.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `argon2id` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`argon2id`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/argon2id.rg` (domain `CRYPTO`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/argon2id.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import argon2id` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/argon2id.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libargon2id.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import argon2id` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 25.2.1. Summary of `argon2id` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `argon2id hash / argon2id verify` | 6-argument RFC 9106 PHC password hashing (`pwd, salt, m_kib, t, p, tag_len`) and constant-time verification |\n| `argon2id hash with preset / argon2id params preset` | Hash using named presets: `\"interactive\"`, `\"owaspfirst\"` (`19456 KiB, t=2`), `\"owaspsecond\"` (`47104 KiB, t=1`), `\"moderate\"`, `\"sensitive\"` |\n| `argon2id hash ctx / argon2id verify ctx` | RFC 9106 hashing with secret `pepper` ($K$) and associated data ($X$) |\n| `argon2id derive key / argon2id needs rehash` | Raw binary key derivation and policy-upgrade detection on stored PHC strings |\n| `blake2b / blake2b hex` | General-purpose RFC 7693 Blake2b cryptographic digest (`1..64` bytes) |\n\n### 25.2.2. Deep Dive into `argon2id`: Step-by-Step Examples for Every Feature\n\nA signature table alone is never enough: let's explore **every capability** of the `argon2id` lib through progressive, runnable Ridgun examples. We will start with two everyday tabular recipes and then walk through every functional scenario, transformation, and boundary condition verified in `src/test/ridgun/test argon2id.rg`.\n\n### 25.3.1. Example 1: Core Tabular Workflow with `argon2id`\n\nNotice how in Ridgun the `=` signs and transformation steps using `argon2id` align into a clean, scannable table without unnecessary mutable variables or hidden side effects:\n\n```ridgun\nimport argon2id\n\nfn :: proteger clave usuario (clave : String) => (String, Bool, Bool)\n      proteger clave usuario clave =\n        phc    = argon2id hash clave \"sal-unica-16byte\" 32 2 1 32\n        valido = argon2id verify clave phc\n        rehash = argon2id needs rehash phc 19456 2 1 32\n        (phc, valido, rehash)\n```\n\n### 25.3.2. c1), z_(2, c),  z_(4, c1), z_(7, c)  = a2 blamka g z_(1, c1) z_(2, c)  z_(4, c1) z_(7, c)\n        z\n```\n\n### 25.4.3. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 18\n\nObserve how `argon2id.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: derive raw tag ctx [Int] [Int] String String Argon2Params => [Int]\n      derive raw tag ctx pwd_raw salt_bytes secret ad params =\n        sec_b = a2 str to bytes secret\n        ad_b  = a2 str to bytes ad\n        m     = m_cost params\n        t     = t_cost params\n        p     = lanes params\n        tlen  = tag_len params\n        h0_in = (a2 u32 to le4 p) :: (a2 u32 to le4 tlen) :: (a2 u32 to le4 m) :: (a2 u32 to le4 t) :: (a2 u32 to le4 19) :: (a2 u32 to le4 2) :: (a2 u32 to le4 (a2 list len pwd_raw)) :: pwd_raw :: (a2 u32 to le4 (a2 list len salt_bytes)) :: salt_bytes :: (a2 u32 to le4 (a2 list len sec_b)) :: sec_b :: (a2 u32 to le4 (a2 list len ad_b)) :: ad_b\n        h0    = a2 blake2b h0_in 64\n        sl    = m / (4 * p)\n        q     = sl * 4\n        mem0  = a2 init all lanes h0 0 p q\n        ctx   = A2Ctx 0 0 0 p q sl t\n        memf  = a2 run passes mem0 ctx 0\n        c0    = a2 nth blk memf (q - 1)\n        c_fin = a2 bitxor final lanes memf 1 p q c0\n        a2 blake2b long (a2 blk8 to bytes c_fin) tlen\n\nfn :: derive raw tag [Int] [Int] Argon2Params => [Int]\n      derive raw tag pwd_raw salt_bytes params = derive raw tag ctx pwd_raw salt_bytes \"\" \"\" params\n\n-- Unpadded Base64 Encoding & Decoding (PHC Specification)\n\nfn :: b64 alphabet => String\n      b64 alphabet =  \"ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789+/\"\n\nfn :: b64 char Int => String\n      b64 char idx =  str slice (b64 alphabet) (idx % 64) ((idx % 64) + 1)\n\nfn :: b64 encode nopad [Int]           => String\n      b64 encode nopad []              =  \"\"\n      b64 encode nopad (b0:[])         =\n        c0 = b0 / 4\n        c1 = (b0 % 4) * 16\n        (b64 char c0) + (b64 char c1)\n      b64 encode nopad (b0:b1:[])      =\n        c0 = b0 / 4\n        c1 = ((b0 % 4) * 16) + (b1 / 16)\n        c2 = (b1 % 16) * 4\n        (b64 char c0) + (b64 char c1) + (b64 char c2)\n      b64 encode nopad (b0:b1:b2:rest) =\n        c0 = b0 / 4\n        c1 = ((b0 % 4) * 16) + (b1 / 16)\n        c2 = ((b1 % 16) * 4) + (b2 / 64)\n        c3 = b2 % 64\n        (b64 char c0) + (b64 char c1) + (b64 char c2) + (b64 char c3) + (b64 encode nopad rest)\n```\n\n### 25.4.4. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 26\n\nObserve how `argon2id.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: argon2id needs rehash String Argon2Params => Bool\n      argon2id needs rehash phc    target       =  check rehash parsed (argon2id parse phc phc) target\n\nfn :: verify parsed ctx Argon2PhcRes String String String => Bool\n      verify parsed ctx (Argon2PhcErr _) _ _ _ = False\n      verify parsed ctx (Argon2PhcOk parsed) pwd secret ad =\n        salt_bytes = b64 decode nopad (salt_b64 parsed)\n        tag_bytes  = b64 decode nopad (tag_b64 parsed)\n        computed   = derive raw tag ctx (a2 str to bytes pwd) salt_bytes secret ad (params parsed)\n        constant time bytes eq computed tag_bytes\n\nfn :: argon2id verify ctx String String String String => Bool\n      argon2id verify ctx pwd phc secret ad = verify parsed ctx (argon2id parse phc phc) pwd secret ad\n\nfn :: argon2id verify String String => Bool\n      argon2id verify pwd    phc    =\n        | not (str starts with phc \"$argon2id$\") = False\n        | otherwise                              = argon2id verify ctx pwd phc \"\" \"\"\n\nfn :: unwrap phc res Argon2Res      => String\n      unwrap phc res (Argon2Ok phc) =  phc\n      unwrap phc res (Argon2Err _)  =  \"\"\n\nfn :: argon2id hash String String Int Int Int => String\n      argon2id hash pwd salt m_kib t_iters p_lanes =\n        unwrap phc res (argon2id hash params pwd salt (Argon2Params m_kib t_iters p_lanes 32 19))\n\nfn :: argon2id hash phc String String Int Int Int => String\n      argon2id hash phc pwd salt m_kib t_iters p_lanes =\n        argon2id hash pwd salt m_kib t_iters p_lanes\n```\n\n---\n\n## 25.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `argon2id`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Byte-for-byte match with official RFC 9106 §5.3 and OpenSSL 3.5 test vectors, operating on native `Tensor[U64 * 8 * 16]` blocks without a single line of C.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/argon2id.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test argon2id.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `argon2id`\n\n1. **Declare `import argon2id` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `argon2id` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `argon2id`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `argon2id` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 26**, we will explore the **`jwt`** lib in full depth (The `jwt` Lib — RFC 7519 `HS256` JSON Web Tokens and ChaCha20-Poly1305 Encrypted Tokens).\n"}, {"num": "25", "slug": "26-lib-jwt-rfc7519-and-encrypted-tokens", "file": "26-lib-jwt-rfc7519-and-encrypted-tokens.md", "title": "Chapter 26: The `jwt` Lib — RFC 7519 `HS256` JSON Web Tokens and ChaCha20-Poly1305 Encrypted Tokens", "short_title": "The `jwt` Lib — RFC 7519 `HS256` JSON Web Tokens and ChaCha20-Poly1305 Encrypted Tokens", "markdown": "# Chapter 26: The `jwt` Lib — RFC 7519 `HS256` JSON Web Tokens and ChaCha20-Poly1305 Encrypted Tokens\n\n## 26.1. Why Does the `jwt` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `jwt` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `jwt`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: the JWT ecosystem is notorious for `\"alg\":\"none\"` signature-bypass bugs and for developers stuffing confidential data into a standard JWT while forgetting that its payload is merely Base64url-encoded, not encrypted.\n\nThe **`jwt`** lib solves both needs: it signs and verifies **RFC 7519 (`HS256`) JWTs** while strictly enforcing `alg == \"HS256\"`, constant-time MAC comparison, and `exp`/`nbf` windows, and it also provides **`token seal` / `token open`** for opaque tokens encrypted with **ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD**.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`JWT`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`jwt`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/jwt.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `jwt` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `jwt` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `jwt` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 26.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `jwt` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`jwt`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/jwt.rg` (domain `JWT`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/jwt.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import jwt` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/jwt.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libjwt.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import jwt` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 26.2.1. Summary of `jwt` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `type JwtResult` | `JwtOk JsonVal | JwtExpired | JwtNotYetValid | JwtBadSignature | JwtMalformed` |\n| `jwt sign hs256 / jwt verify hs256` | Sign `JsonVal` claims and verify signature + `alg == \"HS256\"` + `exp`/`nbf` |\n| `jwt claims / jwt get str / jwt get int` | Build standard `(sub, iss, iat, exp)` claims and query verified payloads |\n| `token seal / token open` | Encrypt and authenticate opaque JSON tokens via ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD |\n\n### 26.2.2. \"AC2: verified payload matches input\"\n\n        wrong_sec = jwt verify hs256 tok \"wrong-secret\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok wrong_sec) \"AC2: jwt verify hs256 rejects wrong secret\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt verify hs256 tok \"\")) \"AC2: jwt verify hs256 rejects empty secret\"\n\n        tampered = tok + \"x\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt verify hs256 `jwt` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test_jwt_claims_temporal_validation => ()\n      test_jwt_claims_temporal_validation =\n        claims = JwtClaims \"ridgun-auth\" \"agent-007\" 2000 1000 \"tier=gold\"\n        secret = \"claims-signing-secret\"\n        tok = jwt sign claims claims secret\n\n        -- Exact nbf boundary: now == 1000 is valid (now >= nbf)\n        at_nbf_res = jwt verify claims tok secret 1000\n        _ = assert true (is_claims_ok at_nbf_res) \"AC2: claims valid at exact nbf boundary (now == 1000)\"\n\n        ok_res = jwt verify claims tok secret 1500\n        _ = assert true (is_claims_ok ok_res) \"AC2: claims valid at now=1500\"\n        c_out = unwrap_claims ok_res\n        _ = assert str eq (issuer c_out) \"ridgun-auth\" \"AC2: issuer preserved\"\n        _ = assert str eq (subject c_out) \"agent-007\" \"AC2: subject preserved\"\n (custom_json c_out) \"tier=gold\" \"AC2: custom_json preserved\"\n\n        -- 1 second before exp: now == 1999 is valid\n        before_exp_res = jwt verify claims tok secret 1999\n        _ = assert assert false (is_claims_ok late_res) \"AC2: claims rejected after exp (5000 >= 2000)\"\n        _ = assert false (is_claims_ok (jwt verify claims tok \"wrong-secret\" 1500)) \"AC2: claims rejected Example 5: Encrypted and Authenticated Tokens with ChaCha20-Poly1305 AEAD (`jwt seal aead`, `jwt open aead`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `jwt` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test_jwt_aead_seal_and_open => ()\n      test_jwt_aead_seal_and_open =\n        key32 = \"0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef\"\n        other_key32 = \"fedcba9876543210fedcba9876543210\"\n        nonce12 = \"uniqueNonce1\"\n        other_nonce12 = \"uniqueNonce2\"\n        secret_msg = '{\"api_key\":\"rk_live_998877\",\"scope\":\"full\"}'\n\n        sealed = jwt seal aead key32 nonce12 secret_msg\n        _ = assert true (is_jwt_ok sealed) \"AC3: jwt seal aead succeeds with 32B key and 12B nonce\"\n        tok = unwrap_jwt sealed\n\n        opened = jwt open aead key32 nonce12 tok\n        _ = assert true (is_jwt_ok opened) \"AC3: jwt open aead succeeds\"\n        _ = assert str eq (unwrap_jwt opened) secret_msg \"AC3: jwt open aead round-trips plaintext\"\n\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt open aead other_key32 nonce12 tok)) \"AC3: jwt open aead rejects wrong 32B key\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt open aead key32 other_nonce12 tok)) \"AC3: jwt open aead rejects wrong 12B nonce\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt open aead key32 nonce12 (tok + \"A\"))) \"AC3: jwt open aead rejects tampered ciphertext/tag\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt seal aead \"short\" nonce12 secret_msg)) \"AC3: jwt seal aead rejects non-32B key\"\n        _ = assert false (is_jwt_ok (jwt seal aead key32 \"short\" secret_msg)) \"AC3: jwt seal aead rejects non-12B nonce\"\n        ()\n```\n\n---\n\n## 26.4. Under the Hood: How `jwt` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/jwt.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/jwt.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 26.4.1. Internal Implementation: Byte, Hex, Base64url & Constant-Time Helpers\n\nObserve how `jwt.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: jwt_b64url_lut Int => String\n      jwt_b64url_lut _   =  \"ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789-_\"\n\nfn :: jwt_b64_char Int => String\n      jwt_b64_char idx =\n        tbl = jwt_b64url_lut 0\n        i = ((idx % 64) + 64) % 64\n        str_slice tbl i (i + 1)\n\nfn :: jwt_b64_val Int => Int\n      jwt_b64_val c\n        | c >= 65 AND c <= 90 = c - 65\n        | c >= 97 AND c <= 122 = (c - 97) + 26\n        | c >= 48 AND c <= 57 = (c - 48) + 52\n        | c == 45 OR c == 43 = 62\n        | c == 95 OR c == 47 = 63\n        | otherwise = -1\n\nfn :: jwt_b64url_enc_bytes [Int]         => String\n      jwt_b64url_enc_bytes []            =  \"\"\n      jwt_b64url_enc_bytes (b0:[])       =  (jwt_b64_char (b0 / 4)) + (jwt_b64_char ((b0 % 4) * 16))\n      jwt_b64url_enc_bytes (b0:b1:[])    =\n        n = (b0 * 256) + b1\n        (jwt_b64_char (n / 1024)) + (jwt_b64_char ((n / 16) % 64)) + (jwt_b64_char ((n % 16) * 4))\n      jwt_b64url_enc_bytes (b0:b1:b2:rs) =\n        n = (b0 * 65536) + (b1 * 256) + b2\n        (jwt_b64_char (n / 262144)) + (jwt_b64_char ((n / 4096) % 64)) + (jwt_b64_char ((n / 64) % 64)) + (jwt_b64_char (n % 64)) + (jwt_b64url_enc_bytes rs)\n\nfn :: jwt_str_to_bytes_loop String Int Int [Int] => [Int]\n      jwt_str_to_bytes_loop s      pos len acc\n        | pos >= len = acc\n        | otherwise = jwt_str_to_bytes_loop s (pos + 1) len (acc :: [((str_char_at s pos) % 256 + 256) % 256])\n\nfn :: jwt_str_to_bytes String => [Int]\n      jwt_str_to_bytes s      =  jwt_str_to_bytes_loop s 0 (str_len s) []\n\nfn :: jwt_bytes_to_str [Int]    => String\n      jwt_bytes_to_str []       =  \"\"\n      jwt_bytes_to_str (b:rest) =  (char_from_code b) + (jwt_bytes_to_str rest)\n\nfn :: jwt_b64url_encode String => String\n      jwt_b64url_encode s      =  jwt_b64url_enc_bytes (jwt_str_to_bytes s)\n\nfn :: jwt_b64url_valid_loop String Int Int => Bool\n      jwt_b64url_valid_loop s      pos len\n        | pos >= len = True\n        | (jwt_b64_val (str_char_at s pos)) < 0 = False\n        | otherwise = jwt_b64url_valid_loop s (pos + 1) len\n```\n\n### 26.4.2. Internal Implementation: Constant-Time Equality & Bitwise XOR\n\nObserve how `jwt.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: jwt_bxor_loop Int Int Int Int => Int\n      jwt_bxor_loop _   _   0   acc =  acc\n      jwt_bxor_loop a   b   bit acc\n        | ((a % 2) != (b % 2)) = jwt_bxor_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) (acc + bit)\n        | otherwise = jwt_bxor_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) acc\n\nfn :: jwt_byte_xor Int Int => Int\n      jwt_byte_xor a   b   =  jwt_bxor_loop (((a % 256) + 256) % 256) (((b % 256) + 256) % 256) 1 0\n\nfn :: jwt_ct_diff String String Int Int Int => Int\n      jwt_ct_diff a b idx len acc\n        | idx >= len = acc\n        | otherwise = jwt_ct_diff a b (idx + 1) len (acc + (jwt_byte_xor (str_char_at a idx) (str_char_at b idx)))\n\nfn :: jwt_ct_eq String String => Bool\n      jwt_ct_eq a      b\n        | (str_len a) != (str_len b) = False\n        | otherwise = (jwt_ct_diff a b 0 (str_len a) 0) == 0\n```\n\n### 26.4.3. Internal Implementation: HS256 (HMAC-SHA256) Signing & Verification\n\nObserve how `jwt.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: jwt_hex_nib Int => Int\n      jwt_hex_nib c\n        | c >= 48 AND c <= 57 = c - 48\n        | c >= 97 AND c <= 102 = (c - 97) + 10\n        | c >= 65 AND c <= 70 = (c - 65) + 10\n        | otherwise = 0\n\nfn :: jwt_hex_to_bytes_loop String Int Int [Int] => [Int]\n      jwt_hex_to_bytes_loop h      pos len acc\n        | (pos + 1) >= len = acc\n        | otherwise = jwt_hex_to_bytes_loop h (pos + 2) len (acc :: [((jwt_hex_nib (str_char_at h pos)) * 16) + (jwt_hex_nib (str_char_at h (pos + 1)))])\n\nfn :: jwt_hex_to_bytes String => [Int]\n      jwt_hex_to_bytes h      =  jwt_hex_to_bytes_loop h 0 (str_len h) []\n\nfn :: jwt_pad_key_bytes [Int] Int => [Int]\n      jwt_pad_key_bytes bs    idx\n        | idx >= 64 = []\n        | bs == [] = [0] :: (jwt_pad_key_bytes [] (idx + 1))\n        | otherwise = jwt_pad_key_head bs idx\n\nfn :: jwt_pad_key_head [Int]    Int => [Int]\n      jwt_pad_key_head []       idx =  jwt_pad_key_bytes [] idx\n      jwt_pad_key_head (b:rest) idx =  [b] :: (jwt_pad_key_bytes rest (idx + 1))\n\nfn :: jwt_key_block String => [Int]\n      jwt_key_block secret\n        | (str_len secret) > 64 = jwt_pad_key_bytes (jwt_hex_to_bytes (sha256_str secret)) 0\n        | otherwise = jwt_pad_key_bytes (jwt_str_to_bytes secret) 0\n\nfn :: jwt_xor_pad_str [Int]    Int => String\n      jwt_xor_pad_str []       _   =  \"\"\n      jwt_xor_pad_str (b:rest) pad =\n        xb = jwt_byte_xor b pad\n        safe_c = (xb % 126) + 1\n        (char_from_code safe_c) + (jwt_xor_pad_str rest pad)\n\nfn :: jwt_hmac_sha256_b64url String String        => String\n      jwt_hmac_sha256_b64url secret signing_input =\n        kb = jwt_key_block secret\n        ipad = jwt_xor_pad_str kb 54\n        opad = jwt_xor_pad_str kb 92\n        inner_hex = sha256_str (ipad + signing_input)\n        outer_hex = sha256_str (opad + inner_hex)\n        jwt_b64url_enc_bytes (jwt_hex_to_bytes outer_hex)\n\nfn :: jwt_hs256_header_json Int => String\n      jwt_hs256_header_json _   =  '{\"alg\":\"HS256\",\"typ\":\"JWT\"}'\n```\n\n### 26.4.4. Internal Implementation: Structured Claims Signing & Temporal Validation\n\nObserve how `jwt.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: jwt sign claims JwtClaims String => String\n      jwt sign claims c         secret =\n        base_obj = JsonObj [JsonField \"iss\" (JsonStr (issuer c)), JsonField | (str_len nonce) != 12 = JwtErr \"AEAD nonce must be 12 bytes\"\n        | otherwise = jwt_seal_aead_ok key nonce plaintext\n```\n\n---\n\n## 26.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `jwt`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Complete immunity to `\"alg\":\"none\"` downgrade attacks (any header where `alg != \"HS256\"` returns `JwtBadSignature`) and constant-time MAC verification.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/jwt.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test jwt.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `jwt`\n\n1. **Declare `import jwt` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `jwt` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `jwt`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `jwt` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 27**, we will explore the **`socket`** lib in full depth (The `socket` Lib — TCP, UDP, AF_UNIX, Framing, DNS, and `RGF-001` Kernel Sockets).\n"}, {"num": "26", "slug": "27-lib-socket-tcp-udp-dns-and-kernel-sockets", "file": "27-lib-socket-tcp-udp-dns-and-kernel-sockets.md", "title": "Chapter 27: The `socket` Lib — TCP, UDP, AF_UNIX, Framing, DNS, and `RGF-001` Kernel Sockets", "short_title": "The `socket` Lib — TCP, UDP, AF_UNIX, Framing, DNS, and `RGF-001` Kernel Sockets", "markdown": "# Chapter 27: The `socket` Lib — TCP, UDP, AF_UNIX, Framing, DNS, and `RGF-001` Kernel Sockets\n\n## 27.1. Why Does the `socket` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `socket` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `socket`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: programming BSD sockets in C with `struct sockaddr_in`, `htons`, `inet_pton`, and `getaddrinfo` (which drags in `glibc`'s dynamic `libnss` plugins) is an arcane 1980s ritual.\n\nThe **`socket`** lib provides two complementary zero-C layers: (1) the **`RGF-001`** kernel descriptor API built for production servers like **Solstice** (`sys tcp listen`, `sys tcp listen reuseport`, `sys accept`, `sys tcp connect`, `sys send`, `sys recv`, `sys resolve ipv4` via `/etc/hosts` + RFC 1035 UDP port 53 DNS wire packets) and (2) the immutable functional `Socket` state machine with length-prefixed framing, CRLF line I/O, and CIDR matching.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`SOCK`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`socket`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/socket.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `socket` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `socket` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `socket` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 27.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `socket` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`socket`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/socket.rg` (domain `SOCK`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/socket.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import socket` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/socket.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libsocket.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import socket` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 27.2.1. Summary of `socket` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `sys tcp listen / sys tcp listen reuseport / sys unix listen` | Bind and listen on IPv4 TCP (`SO_REUSEADDR` / `SO_REUSEPORT`) or `AF_UNIX` |\n| `sys accept / sys tcp connect / sys unix connect` | Deadline-bounded accept and connect returning `SockFdResult` / `SockAcceptResult` |\n| `sys send / sys recv / sys shutdown write` | Timeout-bounded kernel socket send, receive, and half-close (`SHUT_WR`) |\n| `sys resolve ipv4 / dns resolve a` | Pure-Ridgun IPv4 resolution via `/etc/hosts` and RFC 1035 UDP DNS wire queries |\n| `sock send frame / sock recv frame / ipv4 in cidr` | Length-prefixed (`N:payload`) framing and IPv4 CIDR subnet matching |\n\n### 27.2.2.
