Deep Dive into `socket`: Step-by-Step Examples for Every Feature\n\nA signature table alone is never enough: let's explore **every capability** of the `socket` lib through progressive, runnable Ridgun examples. We will start with two everyday tabular recipes and then walk through every functional scenario, transformation, and boundary condition verified in `src/test/ridgun/test socket.rg`.\n\n### 27.3.1. Example 1: Core Tabular Workflow with `socket`\n\nNotice how in Ridgun the `=` signs and transformation steps using `socket` align into a clean, scannable table without unnecessary mutable variables or hidden side effects:\n\n```ridgun\nimport socket\n\nfn :: eco tcp local () => String\n      eco tcp local =\n        l_res = sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 0 16\n        lfd   = sock fd l_res\n        port  = sys local port lfd\n        c_res = sys tcp connect \"127.0.0.1\" port 1000\n        cfd   = sock fd c_res\n        a_res = sys accept lfd 1000\n        sfd   = sock accept fd a_res\n        _     = sys send cfd \"hola-solstice\" 1000\n        r_res = sys recv sfd 64 1000\n        datos = sock recv data r_res\n        _     = sys_close cfd\n        _     = sys_close sfd\n        _     = sys_close lfd\n        datos\n```\n\n### 27.3.2. Example 2: Safe Composition and Boundary Handling\n\nEvery function in the `socket` lib is designed to compose cleanly with boolean guards `|` and with the rest of the 32 libs in `ridgun-libs`:\n\n```ridgun\nimport socket\n\nfn :: verificar ip interna (ip : String) => Bool\n      verificar ip interna ip =\n        ipv4 in cidr ip \"10.0.0.0/8\"\n```\n\n### 27.3.3. Example 6: Live Linux x86_64 Kernel Sockets (RGF-001): Loopback TCP Client/Server (`127.0.0.1`) and Unix Domain Sockets (`AF_UNIX`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `socket` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test sock rgf001 kernel tcp and unix => ()\n      test sock rgf001 kernel tcp and unix =\n        -- 1. Ephemeral TCP listen + sys_local_port + ADDR_IN_USE + invalid/permission checks\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys tcp listen \"bad_host\" 8080 16)) (0 - 7) \"invalid host -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 70000 16)) (0 - 7) \"invalid port -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 8080 0)) (0 - 7) \"invalid backlog -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 80 16)) (0 - 6) \"privileged port 80 -> -6\"\n        l_res  = sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 0 16\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst l_res) 0 \"sys_tcp_listen status\"\n        lfd    = tup2 snd l_res\n        _      = assert true (lfd >= 0) \"lfd >= 0\"\n        _      = assert true ((sys call 72 lfd 1 0 0 0 0) >= 0) \"lfd is real kernel fd (fcntl F_GETFD >= 0)\"\n        lport  = sys local port lfd\n        _      = assert true (lport > 0) \"ephemeral port > 0\"\n        dup_l  = sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" lport 8\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst dup_l) (0 - 5) \"dup sys_tcp_listen -> -5\"\n\n        -- 1b. Real DNS resolution (/etc/hosts + RFC 1035 wire packet parser + localhost + literal IP + hostname connect)\n        r_loc  = sys resolve ipv4 \"localhost\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 rc r_loc) 0 \"sys_resolve_ipv4 localhost ok\"\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str r_loc) \"127.0.0.1\" \"sys_resolve_ipv4 localhost -> 127.0.0.1\"\n        r_bad  = sys resolve ipv4 \"-bad..host\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 rc r_bad) (0 - 7) \"sys_resolve_ipv4 invalid hostname -> -7\"\n        dns_q  = sk build dns query \"example.com\"\n        _      = assert int equals (str len dns_q) 29 \"RFC 1035 DNS query length for example.com == 29\"\n        -- Construct an RFC 1035 DNS response packet with 1 A record (93.184.216.34) using 0xC00C name compression\n        dns_hd = (sk raw byte 18) + (sk raw byte 52) + (sk raw byte 129) + (sk raw byte 128) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 0)\n        dns_qn = sk encode qname [\"example\", \"com\"]\n        dns_qt = (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1)\n        dns_an = (sk raw byte 192) + (sk raw byte 12) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 1) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 60) + (sk raw byte 0) + (sk raw byte 4) + (sk raw byte 93) + (sk raw byte 184) + (sk raw byte 216) + (sk raw byte 34)\n        dns_ip = sk extract dns ipv4 (dns_hd + dns_qn + dns_qt + dns_an)\n        _      = assert str eq dns_ip \"93.184.216.34\" \"sk_extract_dns_ipv4 parses RFC 1035 A record (93.184.216.34)\"\n        c_host = sys tcp connect \"localhost\" lport 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst c_host) 0 \"sys_tcp_connect via hostname localhost ok\"\n        a_host = sys accept lfd 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc a_host) 0 \"sys_accept from hostname connect ok\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close (tup2 snd c_host)) 0 \"close c_host\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close (tup3 fd a_host)) 0 \"close a_host\"\n\n        -- 2. Accept timeout (-2) when no client pending + connect refused (-3) on unbound port\n        acc_to = sys accept lfd 30\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc acc_to) (0 - 2) \"sys_accept timeout -> -2\"\n        ref_c  = sys tcp connect \"127.0.0.1\" 39999 100\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst ref_c) (0 - 3) \"sys_tcp_connect refused -> -3\"\n\n        -- 3. TCP multi-client queue, bidirectional I/O, chunked recv, recv timeout (-2), shutdown_write, send after shutdown (-4), EOF (-1)\n        c_res  = sys tcp connect \"127.0.0.1\" lport 500\n        c_res2 = sys tcp connect \"127.0.0.1\" lport 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst c_res) 0 \"sys_tcp_connect 1 ok\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst c_res2) 0 \"sys_tcp_connect 2 ok\"\n        cfd    = tup2 snd c_res\n        cfd2   = tup2 snd c_res2\n        a_res  = sys accept lfd 500\n        a_res2 = sys accept lfd 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc a_res) 0 \"sys_accept 1 ok\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc a_res2) 0 \"sys_accept 2 ok\"\n        sfd    = tup3 fd a_res\n        sfd2   = tup3 fd a_res2\n\n        -- Empty recv with timeout=20 returns (-2, \"\")\n        rcv_to = sys recv sfd 16 20\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 rc rcv_to) (0 - 2) \"sys_recv timeout on idle stream -> -2\"\n\n        -- Chunked recv: send 10 bytes, read 4 + 4 + 2 bytes, plus reverse direction sfd -> cfd\n        snd1   = sys send cfd \"0123456789\" 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst snd1) 0 \"sys_send status\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 snd snd1) 10 \"sys_send len 10\"\n        ch1    = sys recv sfd 4 500\n        ch2    = sys recv sfd 4 500\n        ch3    = sys recv sfd 4 500\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str ch1) \"0123\" \"chunk 1 (4B)\"\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str ch2) \"4567\" \"chunk 2 (4B)\"\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str ch3) \"89\" \"chunk 3 (2B)\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys send sfd \"pong\" 500)) 0 \"server->client send ok\"\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str (sys recv cfd 16 500)) \"pong\" \"client recv pong\"\n\n        -- Raw binary bytes (0, 1, 127, 128, 254, 255) + AEAD ciphertext over TCP sys_send/sys_recv\n        raw_bin = bytes from list [0, 1, 127, 128, 254, 255]\n        _       = assert int equals (str len     = assert int equals (str len r_bin) 6 \"raw binary recv len == 6\"\n        _       = assert int equals (str char at r_bin 0) 0 \"raw binary byte[0] == 0\"\n        _       = assert int equals \"confidential-payload-over-tcp\"\n        _       = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys send cfd2 ct_wire 500)) 0 \"sys_send AEAD ciphertext ok\"\n        rx_wire = tup2 str (sys recv sfd2 128 500)\n \"confidential-payload-over-tcp\" \"decrypted TCP payload matches\"\n\n        -- Shutdown write -> subsequent send returns (-4, -1) RESET, peer recv returns (-1, \"\") EOF\n        _      = assert = assert int equals (sys close sfd) 0 \"sys_close sfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close cfd2) 0 \"sys_close cfd2\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close sfd2) 0 \"sys_close sfd2\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close lfd) 0 \"sys_close lfd\"\n\n        -- Operations on closed fd return -7 (INVALID)\n        _      = assert int equals (sys local port lfd) (0 - 7) \"sys_local_port on closed lfd -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc (sys accept lfd 10)) (0 - 7) \"sys_accept on closed lfd -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys send cfd \"x\" 10)) (0 - 7) \"sys_send on closed cfd -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 rc (sys recv sfd 16 10)) (0 - 7) \"sys_recv on closed sfd -> -7\"\n\n        -- 4. AF_UNIX listen + connect + send + recv + path traversal (-7) + missing listener (-3)\n        u_bad  = sys unix listen \"../traversal.sock\" 8\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst u_bad) (0 - 7) \"sys_unix_listen traversal -> -7\"\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys unix connect \"/tmp/rg_nonexistent_99999.sock\" 50)) (0 - 3) \"sys_unix_connect missing -> -3\"\n        upath  = \"/tmp/rg_sock_rgf001_{sys_getpid}.sock\"\n        ul_res = sys unix listen upath 8\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst ul_res) 0 \"sys_unix_listen ok\"\n        ulfd   = tup2 snd ul_res\n        uc_res = sys unix connect upath 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst uc_res) 0 \"sys_unix_connect ok\"\n        ucfd   = tup2 snd uc_res\n        ua_res = sys accept ulfd 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup3 rc ua_res) 0 \"sys_accept unix ok\"\n        usfd   = tup3 fd ua_res\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst (sys send ucfd \"unix-msg\" 500)) 0 \"sys_send unix ok\"\n        urcv   = sys recv usfd 64 500\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 rc urcv) 0 \"sys_recv unix ok\"\n        _      = assert str eq (tup2 str urcv) \"unix-msg\" \"AF_UNIX sys_recv\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close ucfd) 0 \"sys_close ucfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close usfd) 0 \"sys_close usfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close ulfd) 0 \"sys_close ulfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (shell exec \"rm -f '{upath}'\") 0 \"unlink AF_UNIX socket\"\n\n        -- 5. RG-049: sys_send honours timeout_ms when peer never reads (8 MiB send with timeout_ms=1000)\n        tl_res = sys tcp listen \"127.0.0.1\" 0 8\n        tlfd   = tup2 snd tl_res\n        tlport = sys local port tlfd\n        tc_res = sys tcp connect \"127.0.0.1\" tlport 500\n        tcfd   = tup2 snd tc_res\n        ta_res = sys accept tlfd 500\n        tsfd   = tup3 fd ta_res\n        big8m  = str repeat \"x\" 8000000\n        ts0    = sys time ms\n        tsnd   = sys send tsfd big8m 1000\n        ts1    = sys time ms\n        dt_ms  = ts1 - ts0\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst tsnd) (0 - 2) \"RG-049: sys_send 8 MiB to non-reading peer times out -> -2\"\n        _      = assert true ((tup2 snd tsnd) > 0) \"RG-049: sys_send returns partial bytes sent > 0 on timeout\"\n        _      = assert true (dt_ms >= 800 and dt_ms <= 2500) \"RG-049: sys_send timeout elapsed ~1000 ms\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close tcfd) 0 \"sys_close tcfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close tsfd) 0 \"sys_close tsfd\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close tlfd) 0 \"sys_close tlfd\"\n\n        -- 6. Wish (multi-core): SO_REUSEPORT allows two TCP listeners on the same (ip, port)\n        rp1    = sys tcp listen reuseport \"127.0.0.1\" 0 16\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst rp1) 0 \"sys_tcp_listen_reuseport 1 ok\"\n        rp_fd1 = tup2 snd rp1\n        rp_prt = sys local port rp_fd1\n        rp2    = sys tcp listen reuseport \"127.0.0.1\" rp_prt 16\n        _      = assert int equals (tup2 fst rp2) 0 \"sys_tcp_listen_reuseport 2 on same port ok\"\n        rp_fd2 = tup2 snd rp2\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close rp_fd1) 0 \"sys_close rp_fd1\"\n        _      = assert int equals (sys close rp_fd2) 0 \"sys_close rp_fd2\"\n        ()\n```\n\n---\n\n## 27.4. Under the Hood: How `socket` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/socket.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/socket.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 27.4.1. Internal Implementation: RGF-001 Tuple Accessor Helpers\n\nObserve how `socket.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: sock rc (Int Int) => Int\n      sock rc (rc, _)   =  rc\n\nfn :: sock fd (Int Int) => Int\n      sock fd (_, fd)   =  fd\n\nfn :: sock accept rc (Int Int String) => Int\n      sock accept rc (rc, _, _)       =  rc\n\nfn :: sock accept fd (Int Int String) => Int\n      sock accept fd (_, fd, _)       =  fd\n\nfn :: sock accept ip (Int Int String) => String\n      sock accept ip (_, _, ip)       =  ip\n\nfn :: sock recv rc (Int String) => Int\n      sock recv rc (rc, _)      =  rc\n\nfn :: sock recv data (Int String) => String\n      sock recv data (_, data)    =  data\n```\n\n---\n\n## 27.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `socket`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Record updates using immutable `s with field = val` syntax, zero-`glibc`/`libnss` DNS resolution, and deterministic status codes (`0` OK, `-1` EOF, `-2` TIMEOUT, `-3` REFUSED).\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/socket.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test socket.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `socket`\n\n1. **Declare `import socket` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `socket` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `socket`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `socket` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 28**, we will explore the **`http`** lib in full depth (The `http` Lib — RFC 3986 URIs, RFC 9112 HTTP/1.1 Wire Codec, and Live TCP Client).\n"}, {"num": "27", "slug": "28-lib-http-uris-http11-and-tcp-client", "file": "28-lib-http-uris-http11-and-tcp-client.md", "title": "Chapter 28: The `http` Lib — RFC 3986 URIs, RFC 9112 HTTP/1.1 Wire Codec, and Live TCP Client", "short_title": "The `http` Lib — RFC 3986 URIs, RFC 9112 HTTP/1.1 Wire Codec, and Live TCP Client", "markdown": "# Chapter 28: The `http` Lib — RFC 3986 URIs, RFC 9112 HTTP/1.1 Wire Codec, and Live TCP Client\n\n## 28.1. Why Does the `http` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `http` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `http`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: failing to strip `\\r\\n` from HTTP header values invites CRLF header-injection attacks, while linking `libcurl` just to make a clean HTTP/1.1 `GET` or `POST` call is massive overkill.\n\nThe **`http`** lib implements **RFC 3986 URI** parsing, **RFC 9112 HTTP/1.1** request/response wire formatting and parsing with automatic CRLF sanitization (`VUL-INJ-002`) and `Content-Length` injection, `Bearer` token extraction, and a **live kernel-TCP HTTP/1.1 client** (`http get`, `http post`, `http request tcp`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`HTTP`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`http`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/http.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `http` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `http` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `http` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 28.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `http` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`http`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/http.rg` (domain `HTTP`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/http.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import http` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/http.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libhttp.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import http` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 28.2.1. Summary of `http` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `uri parse / uri scheme / uri host / uri port / uri path / uri query` | Decompose RFC 3986 URIs with automatic default ports (`80` for `http`, `443` for `https`) |\n| `http build request / http parse request` | Format and parse HTTP/1.1 wire requests with CRLF header sanitization |\n| `http build response / http parse response` | Format and parse HTTP/1.1 wire responses with status reason phrases |\n| `http header get / http bearer token` | Case-insensitive header lookup and `Authorization: Bearer ` extraction |\n| `http get / http post / http request tcp` | Execute live HTTP/1.1 calls over kernel TCP sockets with `timeout_ms` |\n\n### 28.2.2. Deep Dive into `http`: Step-by-Step Examples for Every Feature\n\nA signature table alone is never enough: let's explore **every capability** of the `http` lib through progressive, runnable Ridgun examples. We will start with two everyday tabular recipes and then walk through every functional scenario, transformation, and boundary condition verified in `src/test/ridgun/test http.rg`.\n\n### 28.3.1. Example 1: Core Tabular Workflow with `http`\n\nNotice how in Ridgun the `=` signs and transformation steps using `http` align into a clean, scannable table without unnecessary mutable variables or hidden side effects:\n\n```ridgun\nimport http\n\nfn :: inspeccionar endpoint (url : String) => (String, Int, String, String)\n      inspeccionar endpoint url =\n        host = uri host url\n        port = uri port url\n        path = uri path url\n        wire = http build request \"GET\" path [(\"Host\", host)] \"\"\n        (host, port, path, wire)\n```\n\n### 28.3.2. Example 2: Safe Composition and Boundary Handling\n\nEvery function in the `http` lib is designed to compose cleanly with boolean guards `|` and with the rest of the 32 libs in `ridgun-libs`:\n\n```ridgun\nimport http\n\nfn :: extraer credenciales (raw_req : String) => (String, String)\n      extraer credenciales raw_req =\n        (metodo, ruta, _, hdrs_raw, _) = http parse request raw_req\n        hdrs = http parse headers hdrs_raw\n        tok  = http bearer token hdrs\n        (metodo + \" \" + ruta, tok)\n```\n\n### 28.3.3. Example 3: RFC 3986 URI Parsing (`uri scheme`, `uri host`, `uri port`, `uri path`, `uri query`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `http` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test uri parsing => ()\n      test uri parsing =\n        u1 = \"https://api.example.com:8443/v1/users?active=1&role=admin\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri scheme u1) \"https\" \"uri_scheme https\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri host u1) \"api.example.com\" \"uri_host\"\n        _  = assert int equals (uri port u1) 8443 \"uri_port 8443\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri path u1) \"/v1/users\" \"uri_path\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri query u1) \"active=1&role=admin\" \"uri_query\"\n        u2 = \"http://localhost/health\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri scheme u2) \"http\" \"uri_scheme http\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri host u2) \"localhost\" \"uri_host localhost\"\n        _  = assert int equals (uri port u2) 80 \"default http port 80\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri path u2) \"/health\" \"uri_path /health\"\n        _  = assert str eq (uri query u2) \"\" \"uri_query empty\"\n        ()\n```\n\n### 28.3.4. Example 4: HTTP/1.1 Request & Response Formatting, Parsing, and Case-Insensitive Header Lookup (`http format request/response`, `http parse request/response`, `http get header`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `http` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: test http11 request and response => ()\n      test http11 request and response =\n        hdrs    = [(\"Host\", \"api.example.com\"), (\"Authorization\", \"Bearer tok_xyz_987\"), (\"X-Injected\", \"safe\\r\\nEvil: 1\")]\n        raw_req = http build request \"POST\" \"/v1/login\" hdrs \"{\\\"user\\\":\\\"alice\\\"}\"\n        parsed  = http parse request raw_req\n        _       = assert str eq (req method parsed) \"POST\" \"req method POST\"\n        _       = assert str eq (req path parsed) \"/v1/login\" \"req path /v1/login\"\n        _       = assert str eq (req body parsed) \"{\\\"user\\\":\\\"alice\\\"}\" \"req body\"\n        phdrs   = http parse headers raw_req\n        _       = assert str eq (http header get phdrs \"host\") \"api.example.com\" \"case-insensitive host header\"\n        _       = assert str eq (http header get phdrs \"content-length\") \"16\" \"auto Content-Length 16\"\n        _       = assert str eq (http bearer token phdrs) \"tok_xyz_987\" \"http_bearer_token\"\n        _ Under the Hood: How `http` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/http.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. Internal Implementation: Internal String & Case Helpers\n\nObserve how `http.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ht index of loop String String Int Int Int => Int\n      ht index of loop s sub idx slen sublen =\n        | (idx + sublen) > slen                   = 0 - 1\n        | (str slice s idx (idx + sublen)) == sub = idx\n        | otherwise                               = ht index of loop s sub (idx + 1) slen sublen\n\nfn :: ht index of String String => Int\n      ht index of s      sub    =\n        | (str len sub) == 0 = 0\n        | otherwise          = ht index of loop s sub 0 (str len s) (str len sub)\n\nfn :: ht lower char String Int Int => String\n      ht lower char s idx c =\n        | c >= 65 and c <= 90 = char from code (c + 32)\n        | otherwise           = str slice s idx (idx + 1)\n\nfn :: ht to lower loop String Int Int => String\n      ht to lower loop s idx len =\n        | idx >= len = \"\"\n        | otherwise  = (ht lower char s idx (str char at s idx)) + (ht to lower loop s (idx + 1) len)\n\nfn :: ht to lower String => String\n      ht to lower s      =  ht to lower loop s 0 (str len s)\n\nfn :: ht is ws Int => Bool\n      ht is ws c   =  c == 32 or c == 9 or c == 10 or c == 13\n\nfn :: ht trim left String Int Int => Int\n      ht trim left s idx len =\n        | idx >= len                   = len\n        | ht is ws (str char at s idx) = ht trim left s (idx + 1) len\n        | otherwise                    = idx\n\nfn :: ht trim right String Int Int => Int\n      ht trim right s start end_pos =\n        | end_pos <= start                       = start\n        | ht is ws (str char at s (end_pos - 1)) = ht trim right s start (end_pos - 1)\n        | otherwise                              = end_pos\n\nfn :: ht trim String => String\n      ht trim s      =\n        st = ht trim left s 0 (str len s)\n        en = ht trim right s st (str len s)\n        str slice s st en\n```\n\n### 28.4.2. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `http`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Automatic stripping of `\\r` and `\\n` across all header keys and values (`http sanitize header`) to prevent HTTP response-splitting attacks.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/http.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test http.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `http`\n\n1. **Declare `import http` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `http` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `http`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `http` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 29**, we will explore the **`ws`** lib in full depth (The `ws` Lib — RFC 6455 WebSocket Handshake, 16/64-Bit Frame Codec, and XOR Masking).\n"}, {"num": "28", "slug": "29-lib-ws-rfc6455-websocket-protocol", "file": "29-lib-ws-rfc6455-websocket-protocol.md", "title": "Chapter 29: The `ws` Lib — RFC 6455 WebSocket Handshake, 16/64-Bit Frame Codec, and XOR Masking", "short_title": "The `ws` Lib — RFC 6455 WebSocket Handshake, 16/64-Bit Frame Codec, and XOR Masking", "markdown": "# Chapter 29: The `ws` Lib — RFC 6455 WebSocket Handshake, 16/64-Bit Frame Codec, and XOR Masking\n\n## 29.1. Why Does the `ws` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `ws` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `ws`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: the WebSocket protocol (RFC 6455) combines a SHA-1 + Base64 upgrade handshake with variable-length binary framing (7-bit, 16-bit, or 64-bit) and mandatory client-to-server 4-byte XOR payload masking.\n\nThe **`ws`** lib implements full **RFC 6455** in pure Ridgun: `Sec-WebSocket-Accept` derivation (including a self-contained RFC 3174 SHA-1 solely for the handshake GUID), `101 Switching Protocols` HTTP upgrade builders, 7/16/64-bit `WsFrame` wire encoding/decoding, symmetric 4-byte XOR masking, and text/ping/pong/close frame constructors.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`WS`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`ws`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/ws.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `ws` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `ws` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `ws` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 29.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `ws` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`ws`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/ws.rg` (domain `WS`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/ws.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import ws` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/ws.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libws.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import ws` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 29.2.1. Under the Hood: How `ws` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/ws.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/ws.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 29.4.1. Internal Implementation: 32-Bit Unsigned Bitwise Helpers for RFC 3174 SHA-1\n\nObserve how `ws.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ws_u32 Int => Int\n      ws_u32 x   =  ((x % 4294967296) + 4294967296) % 4294967296\n\nfn :: ws_u32_xor_loop Int Int Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_xor_loop _   _   0   acc =  acc\n      ws_u32_xor_loop a   b   bit acc\n        | (a % 2) != (b % 2) = ws_u32_xor_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) (acc + bit)\n        | otherwise = ws_u32_xor_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) acc\n\nfn :: ws_u32_xor Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_xor a   b   =  ws_u32_xor_loop (ws_u32 a) (ws_u32 b) 1 0\n\nfn :: ws_u32_and_loop Int Int Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_and_loop _   _   0   acc =  acc\n      ws_u32_and_loop a   b   bit acc\n        | (a % 2) == 1 AND (b % 2) == 1 = ws_u32_and_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) (acc + bit)\n        | otherwise = ws_u32_and_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) acc\n\nfn :: ws_u32_and Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_and a   b   =  ws_u32_and_loop (ws_u32 a) (ws_u32 b) 1 0\n\nfn :: ws_u32_or_loop Int Int Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_or_loop _   _   0   acc =  acc\n      ws_u32_or_loop a   b   bit acc\n        | (a % 2) == 1 OR (b % 2) == 1 = ws_u32_or_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) (acc + bit)\n        | otherwise = ws_u32_or_loop (a / 2) (b / 2) (bit * 2) acc\n\nfn :: ws_u32_or Int Int => Int\n      ws_u32_or a   b   =  ws_u32_or_loop (ws_u32 a) (ws_u32 b) 1 0\n\nfn :: ws_u32_not Int => Int\n      ws_u32_not a   =  4294967295 - (ws_u32 a)\n\nfn :: ws_pow2 Int => Int\n      ws_pow2 0   =  1\n      ws_pow2 n   =  2 * (ws_pow2 (n - 1))\n\nfn :: ws_rotl32 Int Int => Int\n      ws_rotl32 x   n   =\n        ux = ws_u32 x\n        p_shl = ws_pow2 n\n        p_shr = ws_pow2 (32 - n)\n        ws_u32 ((ux * p_shl) + (ux / p_shr))\n\nfn :: ws_byte_xor Int Int => Int\n      ws_byte_xor a   b   =  (ws_u32_xor (a % 256) (b % 256)) % 256\n```\n\n### 29.4.2. Internal Implementation: List & String Byte Conversion Helpers\n\nObserve how `ws.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ws_list_len [Int]    => Int\n      ws_list_len []       =  0\n      ws_list_len (_:rest) =  1 + (ws_list_len rest)\n\nfn :: ws_nth [Int]    Int => Int\n      ws_nth []       _   =  0\n      ws_nth (x:_)    0   =  x\n      ws_nth (_:rest) idx =  ws_nth rest (idx - 1)\n\nfn :: ws_take [Int]    Int => [Int]\n      ws_take []       _   =  []\n      ws_take (x:rest) n\n        | n <= 0 = []\n        | otherwise = [x] :: (ws_take rest (n - 1))\n\nfn :: ws_drop [Int]    Int => [Int]\n      ws_drop []       _   =  []\n      ws_drop (x:rest) n\n        | n <= 0 = [x] :: rest\n        | otherwise = ws_drop rest (n - 1)\n\nfn :: ws_str_to_bytes_loop String Int Int [Int] => [Int]\n      ws_str_to_bytes_loop s      pos len acc\n        | pos >= len = acc\n        | otherwise = ws_str_to_bytes_loop s (pos + 1) len (acc :: [((str_char_at s pos) % 256 + 256) % 256])\n\nfn :: ws_str_to_bytes String => [Int]\n      ws_str_to_bytes s      =  ws_str_to_bytes_loop s 0 (str_len s) []\n\nfn :: ws_bytes_to_str [Int]    => String\n      ws_bytes_to_str []       =  \"\"\n      ws_bytes_to_str (b:rest) =  (char_from_code b) + (ws_bytes_to_str rest)\n```\n\n### 29.4.3. Internal Implementation: Pure-Ridgun SHA-1 & Base64 for RFC 6455 Handshake\n\nObserve how `ws.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ws_zeroes Int => [Int]\n      ws_zeroes n\n        | n <= 0 = []\n        | otherwise = [0] :: (ws_zeroes (n - 1))\n\nfn :: ws_u32_be4 Int => [Int]\n      ws_u32_be4 w   =\n        uw = ws_u32 w\n        [(uw / 16777216) % 256, (uw / 65536) % 256, (uw / 256) % 256, uw % 256]\n\nfn :: ws_sha1_pad [Int] => [Int]\n      ws_sha1_pad bs    =\n        mlen = ws_list_len bs\n        bit_len = mlen * 8\n        rem = (mlen + 1) % 64\n        z_count = ((56 - Internal Implementation: RFC 6455 XOR Masking & Frame Encoding / Decoding\n\nObserve how `ws.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `ws`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Exact match with the official RFC 6455 handshake vector (`\"dGhlIHNhbXBsZSBub25jZQ==\"` $\\rightarrow$ `\"s3pPLMBiTxaQ9kYGzzhZRbK+xOo=\"`) and automatic XOR unmasking in `ws decode frame`.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/ws.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test ws.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `ws`\n\n1. **Declare `import ws` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `ws` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `ws`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `ws` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 30**, we will explore the **`agent`** lib in full depth (The `agent` Lib — Native Actor Concurrency (`agent`, `send`, `receive`), KV Store, and Rate Limiter).\n"}, {"num": "29", "slug": "30-lib-agent-native-actor-concurrency", "file": "30-lib-agent-native-actor-concurrency.md", "title": "Chapter 30: The `agent` Lib — Native Actor Concurrency (`agent`, `send`, `receive`), KV Store, and Rate Limiter", "short_title": "The `agent` Lib — Native Actor Concurrency (`agent`, `send`, `receive`), KV Store, and Rate Limiter", "markdown": "# Chapter 30: The `agent` Lib — Native Actor Concurrency (`agent`, `send`, `receive`), KV Store, and Rate Limiter\n\n## 30.1. Why Does the `agent` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `agent` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `agent`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: sharing mutable state across threads with `Mutex`, `RwLock`, and condition variables is an endless factory of deadlocks and race conditions.\n\nThe **`agent`** lib builds on Ridgun's **Native Agent Model** (`agent`, `send ... to`, `receive from`, `try receive from`, `stop`) to provide isolated message-passing actors, a concurrent key-value store (`KvStore`), a token-bucket rate limiter (`RateBucket`), and supervision state machines (`AgentHealth`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`AGENT`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`agent`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/agent.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `agent` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `agent` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `agent` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 30.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `agent` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`agent`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/agent.rg` (domain `AGENT`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/agent.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import agent` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/agent.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libagent.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import agent` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 30.2.1. Summary of `agent` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `agent roundtrip / agent drain count` | Spawn native Ridgun actors (`agent`), exchange messages (`send ... to` / `receive from`), and stop cleanly |\n| `kv store new / kv store apply` | Actor-ready immutable key-value store (`KvPut`, `KvGet`, `KvDelete`, `KvClear`) |\n| `rate bucket new / rate bucket allow` | Token-bucket rate limiter returning updated bucket and `(allowed, remaining)` |\n| `sup mark failed / sup should restart` | Supervision policy helpers tracking restart budgets (`max_restarts`) |\n\n### 30.2.2. Under the Hood: How `agent` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/agent.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/agent.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 30.4.1. Internal Implementation: Status & Reply Formatting\n\nObserve how `agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: format agent status AgentStatus        => String\n      format agent status AgentRunning       =  \"running\"\n      format agent status AgentStopped       =  \"stopped\"\n      format agent status (AgentTimedOut ms) =  \"timeout after {ms} ms\"\n      format agent status (AgentFailed   msg) = \"failed: {msg}\"\n\nfn :: format agent reply AgentReply        => String\n      format agent reply (ReplyInt     n)  =  int to str n\n      format agent reply (ReplyStr     s)  =  s\n      format agent reply (ReplyBool True)  =  \"true\"\n      format agent reply (ReplyBool False) =  \"false\"\n      format agent reply ReplyEmpty        =  \"agent outbox empty\"\n      format agent reply ReplyStopped      =  \"agent stopped\"\n      format agent reply (ReplyTimeout ms) =  \"agent timeout after {ms} ms\"\n      format agent reply (ReplyError  msg) =  \"agent error: {msg}\"\n```\n\n### 30.4.2. Internal Implementation: Core Combinators (`agent ask`, `agent try ask`, `agent send batch`, `agent receive count`, `agent map batch`, `agent drain ready`)\n\nObserve how `agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: agent ask Agent A   => B\n      agent ask ag    msg =  ask ag msg\n\nfn :: agent try ask Agent A   => Option\n      agent try ask ag    msg =\n        send msg to ag\n        try receive from ag\n\nfn :: agent send batch Agent [A]      => ()\n      agent send batch _     []       =  ()\n      agent send batch ag    (m:rest) =\n        send m to ag\n        agent send batch ag rest\n\nfn :: agent receive count Agent Int   => [B]\n      agent receive count ag    count =  agent receive count loop ag count []\n\nfn :: agent receive count loop Agent Int [B] => [B]\n      agent receive count loop ag count acc\n        | count <= 0 = acc\n        | otherwise =\n          r = receive from ag\n          agent receive count loop ag (count - 1) (acc :: [r])\n\nfn :: agent map batch Agent [A]  => [B]\n      agent map batch ag    msgs =\n        n = agent list len msgs 0\n        _ = agent send batch ag msgs\n        agent receive count ag n\n\nfn :: agent list len [A]    Int => Int\n      agent list len []     acc =  acc\n      agent list len (_:xs) acc =  agent list len xs (acc + 1)\n\nfn :: agent drain ready Agent => [B]\n      agent drain ready ag    =  agent drain ready loop ag []\n\nfn :: agent drain ready loop Agent [B] => [B]\n      agent drain ready loop ag acc =\n        agent drain step ag (try receive from ag) acc\n\nfn :: agent drain step Agent Option [B] => [B]\n      agent drain step _ None acc = acc\n      agent drain step ag (Some r) acc = agent drain ready loop ag (acc :: [r])\n```\n\n### 30.4.3. Internal Implementation: Stateful Counter Agent (`new counter agent`, `counter add`, `counter get`, `counter reset`)\n\nObserve how `agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ag counter step CounterState Int   => CounterState Int\n      ag counter step st           delta =\n        | delta == (0 - 999999999) = (CounterState 0, 0)\n        | otherwise                =\n          nxt = (cnt_val st) + delta\n          (CounterState nxt, nxt)\n\nfn :: new counter agent Int  => Agent Int Int\n      new counter agent init =  agent (ag counter step) (CounterState init)\n\nfn :: counter add (Agent Int Int) Int   => Int\n      counter add ag              delta =\n        | not (agent alive ag) = 0\n        | otherwise            =\n          _ = send delta to ag\n          receive from ag\n\nfn :: counter get (Agent Int Int) => Int\n      counter get ag              =  counter add ag 0\n\nfn :: counter reset (Agent Int Int) => Int\n      counter reset ag              =  counter add ag (0 - 999999999)\n```\n\n### 30.4.4. Internal Implementation: Stateful In-Memory Key-Value Agent (`new kv agent`, `kv put`, `kv get`, `kv delete`, `kv has`, `kv count`, `kv keys`, `kv clear`)\n\nObserve how `agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: ag kv lookup [KvEntry] String => String\n      ag kv lookup []        _      =  \"\"\n      ag kv lookup (e:rest)  key    =\n        | (kv_k e) == key = kv_v e\n        | otherwise       = ag kv lookup rest key\n\nfn :: ag kv exists [KvEntry] String => Bool\n      ag kv exists []        _      =  False\n      ag kv exists (e:rest)  key    =\n        | (kv_k e) == key = True\n        | otherwise       = ag kv exists rest key\n\nfn :: ag kv put list [KvEntry] String String => [KvEntry]\n      ag kv put list [] key val = [KvEntry key val]\n      ag kv put list (e:rest) key val =\n        | (kv_k e) == key = [KvEntry key val] :: rest\n        | otherwise       = [e] :: (ag kv put list rest key val)\n\nfn :: ag kv del list [KvEntry] String => [KvEntry]\n      ag kv del list [] _ = []\n      ag kv del list (e:rest) key =\n        | (kv_k e) == key = rest\n        | otherwise       = [e] :: (ag kv del list rest key)\n\nfn :: ag kv len [KvEntry] => Int\n      ag kv len []        =  0\n      ag kv len (_:rest)  =  1 + (ag kv len rest)\n\nfn :: ag kv keys join String String => String\n      ag kv keys join k rest_s =\n        | (str len rest_s) == 0 = k\n        | otherwise             = k + \",\" + rest_s\n\nfn :: ag kv keys str [KvEntry] => String\n      ag kv keys str []        =  \"\"\n      ag kv keys str (e:rest)  =  ag kv keys join (kv_k e) (ag kv keys str rest)\n\nfn :: ag kv has str Bool  => String\n      ag kv has str True  =  \"1\"\n      ag kv has str False =  \"0\"\n```\n\n---\n\n## 30.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `agent`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Zero shared mutable memory across actors: every message is transferred by value / Copy-on-Write and `stop` deterministically reclaims the actor's region.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/agent.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test agent.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `agent`\n\n1. **Declare `import agent` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `agent` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `agent`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `agent` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 31**, we will explore the **`pool`** lib in full depth (The `pool` Lib — Bounded Worker Pool, Round-Robin Dispatch, and Work-Stealing).\n"}, {"num": "30", "slug": "31-lib-pool-worker-pool-and-work-stealing", "file": "31-lib-pool-worker-pool-and-work-stealing.md", "title": "Chapter 31: The `pool` Lib — Bounded Worker Pool, Round-Robin Dispatch, and Work-Stealing", "short_title": "The `pool` Lib — Bounded Worker Pool, Round-Robin Dispatch, and Work-Stealing", "markdown": "# Chapter 31: The `pool` Lib — Bounded Worker Pool, Round-Robin Dispatch, and Work-Stealing\n\n## 31.1. Why Does the `pool` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `pool` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `pool`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: spawning a fresh thread or actor for every incoming request with no upper bound guarantees that a traffic spike will drive your server into Out-Of-Memory in seconds.\n\nThe **`pool`** lib provides a bounded task dispatcher with **explicit backpressure** (`PoolAccept`, `PoolRejectFull`), balanced $K$-lane batch partitioning (`pool partition`), **work-stealing** queue rebalancing (`pool steal rebalance`), and parallel native-actor execution.\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`AGENT`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`pool`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/pool.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `pool` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `pool` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `pool` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 31.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `pool` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`pool`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/pool.rg` (domain `AGENT`). When built with `stable bundle`, it compiles into three native artifacts:\n\n| Artifact | Path | Purpose |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Ridgun Bundle** | `target/bundles/pool.rglib` | Portable distribution and direct `import pool` resolution |\n| **Compiled Archive** | `target/pool.rgc` | Type-checked intermediate artifact verified by `ridgunc` |\n| **ELF64 Shared Object** | `target/libpool.so` | Native dynamic shared library with zero C or `libc` dependencies |\n\n> To use this lib in any `.rg` file, simply add `import pool` at the top and call its functions using either multi-word spaces (`my fn x`) or underscores (`my_fn x`).\n\n### 31.2.1. Summary of `pool` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `pool new : Int -> Int -> PoolState` | Create a bounded pool with `workers` lanes and `max_queue` capacity |\n| `pool submit : PoolState -> PoolTask -> (PoolState, PoolDecision)` | Enqueue a task round-robin or return `PoolRejectFull` when saturated |\n| `pool partition / pool steal rebalance` | Split batches across $K$ workers and steal half of an overloaded queue for an idle worker |\n| `pool map sum / pool parallel sum2` | Dispatch integer workloads across concurrent native `agent` workers |\n\n### 31.2.2. Under the Hood: How `pool` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/pool.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/pool.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 31.4.1. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 1\n\nObserve how `pool.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\ntype AgentPool = Pool Int [Agent]\n\nfn :: pool size AgentPool   => Int\n      pool size (Pool sz _) =  sz\n\nfn :: pool agents AgentPool    => [Agent]\n      pool agents (Pool _ ags) =  ags\n\n-- Look up the i-th agent in a pool (0-indexed)\nfn :: pool get agent [Agent]  Int => Agent\n      pool get agent (ag:_)   0   =  ag\n      pool get agent (_:rest) idx =  pool get agent rest (idx - 1)\n\nfn :: pool abs Int => Int\n      pool abs x\n        | x < 0     = 0 - x\n        | otherwise = x\n\n-- Route a message to a deterministic shard `key_hash % size` and return the reply via `ask`\nfn :: pool ask shard AgentPool Int A => B\n      pool ask shard (Pool size agents) key_hash msg =\n        shard_idx = (pool abs key_hash) % size\n        target_ag = pool get agent agents shard_idx\n        ask target_ag msg\n\n-- Broadcast a message to every agent in the pool (e.g. configuration reload or tick)\nfn :: pool broadcast AgentPool       A   => ()\n      pool broadcast (Pool _ agents) msg =  pool broadcast list agents msg\n\nfn :: pool broadcast list [Agent]   A   => ()\n      pool broadcast list []        _   =  ()\n      pool broadcast list (ag:rest) msg =\n        send msg to ag\n        pool broadcast list rest msg\n```\n\n### 31.4.2. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 2\n\nObserve how `pool.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: pool drain ready Agent => [B]\n      pool drain ready ag    =  pool drain ready loop ag []\n\nfn :: pool drain ready loop Agent [B] => [B]\n      pool drain ready loop ag acc =\n        pool drain step ag (try receive from ag) acc\n\nfn :: pool drain step Agent Option [B] => [B]\n      pool drain step _ None acc = acc\n      pool drain step ag (Some r) acc = pool drain ready loop ag (acc :: [r])\n\n-- Drain all ready responses across all shards in the pool (non-blocking)\nfn :: pool drain all AgentPool       => [B]\n      pool drain all (Pool _ agents) =  pool drain list agents []\n\nfn :: pool drain list [Agent]   [B] => [B]\n      pool drain list []        acc =  acc\n      pool drain list (ag:rest) acc =\n        ready = pool drain ready ag\n        pool drain list rest (acc :: ready)\n\nfn :: pool stop list [Agent]   => ()\n      pool stop list []        =  ()\n      pool stop list (ag:rest) =\n        stop ag\n        pool stop list rest\n\n-- Stop all agents in the pool and reclaim their isolated state\nfn :: pool shutdown AgentPool       => ()\n      pool shutdown (Pool _ agents) =  pool stop list agents\n```\n\n---\n\n## 31.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `pool`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Strictly bounded queue capacity (`queued <= max_queue`) to protect memory under load spikes and deterministic round-robin / work-stealing distribution.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/pool.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test pool.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `pool`\n\n1. **Declare `import pool` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `pool` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `pool`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `pool` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nIn **Chapter 32**, we will explore the **`net_agent`** lib in full depth (The `net_agent` Lib — Socket Connection Actor Supervisor and Non-Blocking Event Bridge).\n"}, {"num": "31", "slug": "32-lib-net-agent-connection-supervisor", "file": "32-lib-net-agent-connection-supervisor.md", "title": "Chapter 32: The `net_agent` Lib — Socket Connection Actor Supervisor and Non-Blocking Event Bridge", "short_title": "The `net_agent` Lib — Socket Connection Actor Supervisor and Non-Blocking Event Bridge", "markdown": "# Chapter 32: The `net_agent` Lib — Socket Connection Actor Supervisor and Non-Blocking Event Bridge\n\n## 32.1. Why Does the `net_agent` Lib Exist? (The Real-World Problem & Ridgun Philosophy)\n\n> *\"Why do I need a `net_agent` lib written from scratch in pure Ridgun instead of just linking a legacy C library?\"*\n\nThat is the first question every systems engineer asks when meeting `net_agent`. And the answer captures the entire philosophy of `ridgun-libs`:\n\nLet's be honest: in a real server like **Solstice**, accepting TCP connections is the easy part; the hard part is enforcing max concurrent clients, reaping idle zombie connections, and bridging `ppoll` readiness into actor messages without leaking file descriptors.\n\nThe **`net_agent`** lib closes the loop of `ridgun-libs` by uniting **`posix`**, **`socket`**, and **`agent`**: it manages supervised connection tables (`NetSupervisor`), enforces concurrency limits (`max_conns`), reaps idle peers (`net evict idle`), bridges kernel descriptor readiness into typed actor events (`NetEvReadyRead`, `NetEvReadyWrite`, `NetEvTimeout`, `NetEvError`), and provides an end-to-end supervised TCP echo step (`net tcp echo step`).\n\nIn a traditional ecosystem, solving **`AGENT`** tasks means pulling in external C dependencies, wrestling with raw pointers, opaque type conversions, unchecked exceptions, or unpredictable garbage-collector pauses. In Ridgun, the **`net_agent`** lib (`src/main/ridgun/lib/net_agent.rg`) tackles the problem with four non-negotiable principles:\n\n1. **Total Functions & Explicit Contracts (`=>`)**: no function in `net_agent` throws hidden exceptions or relies on `null`. Every edge case (empty inputs, zero divisors, truncated frames, or out-of-bounds indices) returns a documented sentinel value or an algebraic sum type (`Option`, `Result`).\n2. **Zero C Code & Zero `libc`**: every line of `net_agent` compiles directly into native ELF64 machine code verified by `ridgunc`.\n3. **Tail-Call Optimization (TCO)**: all internal loops in `net_agent` are tail-recursive and compile into native hardware loops using $O(1)$ stack space.\n4. **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1` / `RMM2`)**: every intermediate allocation is reclaimed at the exact instruction where its last read finishes, with no Garbage Collector.\n\n---\n\n## 32.2. Native Artifacts, Imports, and Complete `net_agent` API Catalog\n\nIn `ridgun-libs`, **`net_agent`** is a first-class **lib** located at `src/main/ridgun/lib/net_agent.rg` (domain `AGENT`). Summary of `net_agent` Functional Families\n\n| Signature / Function / Type | Purpose & Usage |\n|---|---|\n| `net supervisor new : Int -> Int -> NetSupervisor` | Create a connection supervisor with `max_conns` and `idle_timeout_ms` |\n| `net register conn / net record rx / net record tx / net close conn` | Register accepted sockets, track byte counters/activity timestamps, and close peers |\n| `net evict idle : NetSupervisor -> Int -> (NetSupervisor, [Int])` | Close and evict connections idle longer than `idle_timeout_ms`, returning evicted FDs |\n| `net poll fd : Int -> Bool -> Int -> NetEvent` | Non-blocking `wait fd` bridge emitting `NetEvReadyRead`, `NetEvReadyWrite`, `NetEvTimeout`, or `NetEvError` |\n| `net tcp echo step : String -> Int -> (Int, String)` | End-to-end loopback TCP listen/connect/accept/supervise/echo verification step |\n\n### 32.2.2. \"host,port\" \"kv keys\"\n        _   = assert str eq (kv put kv \"port\" \"9090\") \"9090\" \"kv overwrite port\"\n        _   = assert str eq (kv get kv \"port\") \"9090\" \"kv get updated port\"\n Example 6: Non-Blocking Descriptor Polling (`conn poll step`), Connection Table Sweep (`reactor sweep`), and Graceful Shutdown (`reactor shutdown`)\n\nIn this hands-on walkthrough we thoroughly exercise how `net_agent` behaves across this functional area, covering standard inputs, parameter combinations, and corner cases:\n\n```ridgun\nimport agent\nimport net_agent\n\nfn :: sesion eco (n : Int) (trama : String) => (Int, String)\n      sesion eco n trama =\n        sig = n + 1\n        (sig, \"[#{sig}] {trama}\")\n\nfn :: ejemplo reactor conexiones (fd1 : Int) (fd2 : Int) => [ConnSlot]\n      ejemplo reactor conexiones fd1 fd2 =\n        ag1     = agent sesion eco 0\n        ag2     = agent sesion eco 0\n        activas = [ConnSlot fd1 ag1, ConnSlot fd2 ag2]\n        -- Barrido no bloqueante (sys_poll timeout=0) de todas las conexiones\n        vivas   = reactor sweep activas\n        -- Cierre ordenado de descriptores y parada determinista de agentes\n        _       = reactor shutdown vivas\n        []\n```\n\n---\n\n## 32.4. Under the Hood: How `net_agent` Is Built in Pure Ridgun (`src/main/ridgun/lib/net_agent.rg`)\n\nOne of the biggest advantages of `ridgun-libs` is that **there are no C black boxes**. You can open `src/main/ridgun/lib/net_agent.rg` and read exactly how every operation is implemented using pattern matching, `|` / `|.` guard trees, and tail-call optimization (TCO). Let's inspect the key building blocks of its real implementation:\n\n### 32.4.1. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 1\n\nObserve how `net_agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\ntype ConnSlot = ConnSlot Int Agent\n\ntype StepOutcome = ConnKeep ConnSlot String | ConnClosed\n\n-- Poll a single connection fd (non-blocking, timeout = 0) and route incoming data to its Agent\nfn :: conn poll step ConnSlot         => StepOutcome\n      conn poll step (ConnSlot fd ag) =\n        ev = sys poll fd 1 0\n        conn handle event fd ag ev\n\nfn :: conn handle event Int Agent Int => StepOutcome\n      conn handle event fd ag ev\n        | ev < 0 =\n          -- Poll error: close descriptor and stop agent\n          _ = sys close fd\n          stop ag\n          ConnClosed\n        | ev >= 8 =\n          -- POLLERR (8) or POLLHUP (16): peer disconnected\n          _ = sys close fd\n          stop ag\n          ConnClosed\n        | ev == 0 =\n          -- No new bytes to read; check if the agent has a queued async reply\n      conn drain outbound fd ag (try receive from ag)\n        | otherwise =\n          -- POLLIN (1): read frame and forward to the connection's stateful agent\n          payload = sys read fd fd 65536\n          conn feed payload fd ag payload\n\nfn :: conn feed payload Int Agent String => StepOutcome\n      conn feed payload fd ag \"\" =\n        -- EOF on socket: close descriptor and stop agent deterministically\n        _ = sys close fd\n        stop ag\n        ConnClosed\n      conn feed payload fd ag data =\n        send data to ag\n        conn drain outbound fd ag (try receive from ag)\n```\n\n### 32.4.2. Internal Implementation: Core Implementation Part 2\n\nObserve how `net_agent.rg` declares explicit `fn :: ... => ...` contracts first and resolves each case through aligned equations and tail calls:\n\n```ridgun\nfn :: conn cast str A => String\n      conn cast str x =  x\n\nfn :: conn drain outbound Int Agent Option => StepOutcome\n      conn drain outbound fd ag None = ConnKeep (ConnSlot fd ag) \"\"\n      conn drain outbound fd ag (Some resp) =\n        s = conn cast str resp\n        _ = sys write fd fd s\n        ConnKeep (ConnSlot fd ag) s\n\n-- Sweep an entire connection table, pruning closed connections and keeping active ones\nfn :: reactor sweep [ConnSlot]  => [ConnSlot]\n      reactor sweep []          =  []\n      reactor sweep (slot:rest) =\n        reactor sweep next (conn poll step slot) (reactor sweep rest)\n\nfn :: reactor sweep next StepOutcome       [ConnSlot] => [ConnSlot]\n      reactor sweep next ConnClosed        kept       =  kept\n      reactor sweep next (ConnKeep slot _) kept       =  [slot] :: kept\n\n-- Gracefully close all sockets and stop all connection agents on server shutdown\nfn :: reactor shutdown [ConnSlot] => ()\n      reactor shutdown []         =  ()\n      reactor shutdown ((ConnSlot fd ag):rest) =\n        _ = sys close fd\n        stop ag\n        reactor shutdown rest\n```\n\n---\n\n## 32.5. Algorithmic Guarantees, `RMM1`/`RMM2` Memory, and the 5 Golden Rules of `net_agent`\n\n- **Algorithmic & Safety Guarantee**: Automatic protection against descriptor exhaustion (`max_conns`), deterministic eviction of idle peers, and guaranteed socket cleanup in `net tcp echo step`.\n- **$O(1)$ Stack Complexity via TCO**: Every recursive loop in `src/main/ridgun/lib/net_agent.rg` is in tail position, so `ridgunc` compiles them into native jumps with zero stack growth.\n- **Deterministic Memory (`RMM1`/`RMM2`)**: Zero garbage-collector pauses; all intermediate allocations are reclaimed deterministically on scope exit.\n- **Continuous Verification**: Statically checked via `stable check` and tested by the automated suite in `src/test/ridgun/test net_agent.rg`.\n\n### The 5 Golden Rules When Programming with `net_agent`\n\n1. **Declare `import net_agent` at the top of your file** and take advantage of space/underscore equivalence so your code reads like natural prose.\n2. **Align your data pipelines with `=`**: chain `net_agent` calls into named intermediate steps and let `stable format` align the columns.\n3. **Leverage `net_agent`'s total functions inside `|` guards**: inspect return values or unwrap ADTs via pattern matching rather than assuming external input is valid.\n4. **Avoid cloning large structures when only reading**: remember that under `RMM2`, function input arguments incur zero reference-count overhead (`+0` / `-0`).\n5. **Pair `net_agent` with `test` (`import test`)**: always lock in your domain invariants with `assert equals` or `assert true`.\n\n---\n\nYou have completed the comprehensive tour of all **32 standard libs in `ridgun-libs`**! You now have every building block needed to craft complete, zero-`libc` applications and systems tools in pure Ridgun.\n"}];
    const UI_STRINGS = {"copy_label": "Copy", "copied_label": "Copied!", "prev_label": "← Previous chapter", "next_label": "Next chapter →", "search_hits_label": "Results for", "search_empty_label": "No matches found for"};
    let currentIdx = 0;

    function escapeHtml(s) {
      return s.replace(/&/g, "&").replace(//g, ">");
    }

    function highlightRidgun(code) {
      return code.split("\n").map(line => {
        const comIdx = (() => {
          let inD = false, inS = false;
          for (let i = 0; i < line.length - 1; i++) {
            if (line[i] === "\\") { i++; continue; }
            if (line[i] === '"' && !inS) inD = !inD;
            else if (line[i] === "'" && !inD) inS = !inS;
            else if (!inD && !inS && line[i] === "-" && line[i+1] === "-") return i;
          }
          return -1;
        })();
        const codePart = comIdx >= 0 ? line.slice(0, comIdx) : line;
        const comPart = comIdx >= 0 ? line.slice(comIdx) : "";
        let html = escapeHtml(codePart);
        html = html.replace(/("([^"\\]|\\.)*"|'[^']*')/g, '$1');
        html = html.replace(/\b(fn|op|type|import|mut|otherwise|with|region|timeout|do|global|context|shared|atomic|hardware|echo|and|or|xor|not|agent|send|to|receive|from|try|stop)\b/g, '$1');
        html = html.replace(/\b(Int|Real|String|Char|Bool|U64|Tensor|Option|Result|Some|None|Ok|Err|OutOfMemory|Timeout|True|False)\b/g, '$1');
        html = html.replace(/(::|=>|->|<-|\|\|\.|\|\||\|\.)/g, '$1');
        html = html.replace(/\b(0x[0-9a-fA-F_]+|[0-9][0-9_]*(_KB|_MB|_GB)?(\.[0-9]+)?)\b/g, '$1');
        if (comPart) {
          html += '' + escapeHtml(comPart) + '';
        }
        return html;
      }).join("\n");
    }

    function formatInline(text) {
      let out = escapeHtml(text);
      out = out.replace(/`([^`]+)`/g, "$1");
      out = out.replace(/\*\*([^*]+)\*\*/g, "$1");
      out = out.replace(/\*([^*]+)\*/g, "$1");
      out = out.replace(/\$O\(([^)]+)\)\$/g, "O($1)");
      out = out.replace(/\$\\rightarrow\$/g, "→");
      return out;
    }

    function renderMarkdown(md) {
      const lines = md.split("\n");
      let html = "";
      let i = 0;
      while (i < lines.length) {
        const line = lines[i];
        if (line.startsWith("```")) {
          const lang = line.slice(3).trim() || "ridgun";
          i++;
          const buf = [];
          while (i < lines.length && !lines[i].startsWith("```")) {
            buf.push(lines[i]);
            i++;
          }
          i++;
          const rawCode = buf.join("\n");
          const bodyHtml = (lang === "ridgun" || lang === "") ? highlightRidgun(rawCode) : escapeHtml(rawCode);
          html += `
    ${escapeHtml(lang)}
    ${bodyHtml}
    `;
          continue;
        }
        if (/^---+$/.test(line.trim())) {
          html += "
    ";
          i++;
          continue;
        }
        if (line.startsWith("# ")) {
          html += `
    ${formatInline(line.slice(2).trim())}
    `;
          i++;
          continue;
        }
        if (line.startsWith("## ")) {
          html += `
    ${formatInline(line.slice(3).trim())}
    `;
          i++;
          continue;
        }
        if (line.startsWith("### ")) {
          html += `
    ${formatInline(line.slice(4).trim())}
    `;
          i++;
          continue;
        }
        if (line.trim().startsWith("|") && i + 1 < lines.length && /^\|[\s:-]+\|/.test(lines[i+1].trim())) {
          const headers = line.trim().slice(1, -1).split("|").map(c => formatInline(c.trim()));
          i += 2;
          const rows = [];
          while (i < lines.length && lines[i].trim().startsWith("|")) {
            rows.push(lines[i].trim().slice(1, -1).split("|").map(c => formatInline(c.trim())));
            i++;
          }
          html += '
    ' + headers.map(h => ``).join("") + '' +
                  rows.map(r => '' + r.map(c => ``).join("") + '').join("") + '
    ${h}
    ${c}
    ';
          continue;
        }
        if (line.startsWith(">")) {
          const qLines = [];
          while (i < lines.length && lines[i].startsWith(">")) {
            qLines.push(lines[i].replace(/^>\s?/, ""));
            i++;
          }
          html += `
    ${qLines.map(formatInline).join("
    ")}
    `;
          continue;
        }
        if (/^\s*-\s+/.test(line)) {
          const items = [];
          while (i < lines.length && /^\s*-\s+/.test(lines[i])) {
            items.push(formatInline(lines[i].replace(/^\s*-\s+/, "")));
            i++;
          }
          html += "
      " + items.map(it => `
    • ${it}
      • `).join("") + "
    ";
          continue;
        }
        if (/^\s*\d+\.\s+/.test(line)) {
          const items = [];
          while (i < lines.length && /^\s*\d+\.\s+/.test(lines[i])) {
            items.push(formatInline(lines[i].replace(/^\s*\d+\.\s+/, "")));
            i++;
          }
          html += "
      " + items.map(it => `
    1. ${it}
      2. `).join("") + "
    ";
          continue;
        }
        if (!line.trim()) {
          i++;
          continue;
        }
        const pLines = [];
        while (i < lines.length && lines[i].trim() && !lines[i].startsWith("#") && !lines[i].startsWith("```") && !lines[i].startsWith(">") && !lines[i].trim().startsWith("|") && !/^---+$/.test(lines[i].trim()) && !/^\s*(-|\d+\.)\s+/.test(lines[i])) {
          pLines.push(formatInline(lines[i]));
          i++;
        }
        html += `
    ${pLines.join(" ")}
    `;
      }
      return html;
    }

    window.copyCode = function(btn) {
      const pre = btn.closest(".code-wrapper").querySelector("pre");
      const raw = decodeURIComponent(pre.getAttribute("data-raw"));
      navigator.clipboard.writeText(raw);
      const old = btn.textContent;
      btn.textContent = UI_STRINGS.copied_label;
      setTimeout(() => { btn.textContent = old; }, 1400);
    };

    function renderSidebar() {
      const nav = document.getElementById("navList");
      nav.innerHTML = CHAPTERS.map((ch, idx) => `
        
  • 
          
        
    • 
      `).join("");
    }

    window.selectChapter = function(idx) {
      currentIdx = idx;
      const ch = CHAPTERS[idx];
      location.hash = ch.slug;
      renderSidebar();
      let pagerHtml = '
    ';
      if (idx > 0) {
        pagerHtml += ``;
      } else {
        pagerHtml += '
    ';
      }
      if (idx + 1 < CHAPTERS.length) {
        pagerHtml += ``;
      }
      pagerHtml += '
    ';
      document.getElementById("chapterContent").innerHTML = renderMarkdown(ch.markdown) + pagerHtml;
      window.scrollTo({ top: 0, behavior: "smooth" });
    };

    document.getElementById("searchInput").addEventListener("input", (e) => {
      const q = e.target.value.trim().toLowerCase();
      if (q.length < 2) {
        selectChapter(currentIdx);
        return;
      }
      const hits = [];
      CHAPTERS.forEach((ch, idx) => {
        const lines = ch.markdown.split("\n");
        for (const l of lines) {
          if (l.toLowerCase().includes(q)) {
            hits.push({ idx, title: ch.title, snippet: l.replace(/^[#>*-]+\s*/, "").slice(0, 140) });
            break;
          }
        }
      });
      const box = hits.length
        ? `
    ${escapeHtml(UI_STRINGS.search_hits_label)} "${escapeHtml(q)}" (${hits.length}):` +
          hits.map(h => `
    ${escapeHtml(h.title)}
    ${escapeHtml(h.snippet)}
    `).join("") +
          `
    `
        : `
    ${escapeHtml(UI_STRINGS.search_empty_label)} "${escapeHtml(q)}".
    `;
      document.getElementById("chapterContent").innerHTML = box + renderMarkdown(CHAPTERS[currentIdx].markdown);
    });

    document.getElementById("themeBtn").addEventListener("click", () => {
      const root = document.documentElement;
      root.setAttribute("data-theme", root.getAttribute("data-theme") === "dark" ? "light" : "dark");
    });

    const initialSlug = location.hash.replace(/^#/, "");
    const foundIdx = CHAPTERS.findIndex(c => c.slug === initialSlug);
    selectChapter(foundIdx >= 0 ? foundIdx : 0);
